It was tho mtcntion.of tho Editor to prepare a work 
that should contain a particular description of tho objects in 
Mount Auburn. Ho was afterwards persuaded that this 
could not bo so interesting as certain themes suggested hj 
these objects, and having a general application to burial 
customs. Tho details which havo been omitted were moro 
suitable to a work intended as a guide-book to Mount 
Auburn. Similar matters, compiled In a judicious and in- 
teresting manner, are published weekly in ** The Mount 
Aidmm Memorial" '— ti journal conducted with excellent 
taste and judgment by Mr. Saflbrd. As announced in our 
Prospectus, it was also a secondary object of this work to 
offer incidental remarks on tho general principles of taste, 
that should gorern the artist and proprietor in the construc- 
tion of monuments and fences, in the planting of trees, shrubs, 
and flowers, and in the general disposition of all these ob- 
jects. We haTc ondearorcd to avoid all uninteresting details ; 
for it is designed that tho work shall not possess entirely a 
local or temporary interest, but shall be instructire to many, 
and affordUhcmss of consolatory reflection' to all.

oiif] mcxk'm tUnen^ 1 have Ihx'h lilnirk \viU» one remark- 
able ditfeit'iiec Ijctwtjeii tWm, Those of tlio andetita 
seem more geiicmn)* to liovi> been built for tlic purpose 
of exciting tUe sentiment of atlmiration, wbile tboac of 
^ic modtTiis appeal rattier to otir itk-os of Atnesa and 
prupricty, Jlon displajccl their patriotism in the early 
ages, hy consenting to give their hilwr for the construc- 
tion of some vast work that bIiouM proclaim their 
national gix^tnc&s to the rest of the world. It was no 
msittGr whether thi^ grcjit building was devoted to tho 
living or to the dead, to the purposes of religion or of 
war, if it was only of sufficient magnitude to excito the 
astonifihmcnt of mankind* Tho works of the ancients 
arc diatiiiguishcdf therefore, by their cost and magnifi- 
cence. Their temples, their palaces, and their tombs, ht 
surpassed tlio same class of structures erected in modem 
tim(^ ; and tlio more remote the date of these Ancient 
works, the more stujicnduus and costly do they appear 
to be made. Th© inference to be drawn from tli<^e facta

is, tbiki tlie sentiment of admiration is fully developed in 
the SftTAge and tlio barbarian, and tliat it precedes tlie 
development of those finer sentiments tliat distinguish 
tlie civilized man. 

But though man in a barbarous state can deeply 
adniire certain works and objects, his admiration must . 
be excited by sometliing massive, stupendous, and indica- 
tive of great physical power. To arouse tliis feeling, 
sculpture must be colossal, architecture must vie with 
tlie mountains and emulate the skies, or sink dee])1y into 
the recesses of the earth. Intellect he cannot appreciate, 
except as it is manifested by its control over largo masses 
of material objects. Ilonce the monarchs of ancient 
nations sought tlie reverence of their subjects, not only 
by tlie display of magnificent trophies of war, and costly 
and splendid temples and palaces, but also by tlie vast- 
ness and magnificence of their tombs. The sepulchres 
of Thebes and tlie pyramids of £g)'pt could not be built 
in the present age. The intelligeiTce of modern times 
would revolt at any such sacrifice of the labor of men 
for the erection of works, which could serve no better 
purpose tlian to excite the awe and wonder of an 
ignorant populace. Men are not more utilitarian in 
tlieir intellectual habits than in former times; but the 
public will not tolerate tlie follies of ancient despotisms ; 
and tlie masses, being more intelligent, will not submit 
to being employed in labor for which they receive no 
just recompense. The privileged classes know, that if 
they could afford to build such works, they would be- 
come a tlieme of ridicule and not of admiration. 

Hence, as tlie world has advanced in intelligence, the 
magnificence of all such structures has proportionally 
diminislied. The period tliat preceded the invention of 
writingi or the age of liieroglyphics, was the epoch of 



r„

vast temples, palaces, and tombs, and of colossal statuary. 
During tliis era were built the temples of Tentyra, the 
pyramids of Egypt, tlie sepulchres of Tliebes, and otlier 
vast works which cause us to wonder no more at the 
genius and perfection of art manifested in them, and at 
tlie immense labor and cost that must have attended their 
construction, tlian at the stupidity of the people who 
could thus slavishly do tlie will of the projectore of these 
works. 

The next epoch was that which succeeded the inven- 
tion of letters, before the art of printing was discovered. 
The tombs of diis period wero less magnificent, though 
the priests and monarchs still sustained their sway over 
the minds of the people, by appealing to their sentiment 
of admiration in the greatness of tlicir temples and the 
trophies and statues which were deposited in thems The 
ruling classes were intelligent and enlightened by science, 
as in Greece and Rome, though the masses had not risen 
above the level of the barbarous nations. In this age 
the relics of the dead were burned, and their aslie^ 
dciK)6itcd in urns. Tombs were, tliorefore, less revolt- 
ing, and tlio burial places of friends were held more 
sacred than by the barbarous nations, who buried their 
dead in sepulchres and pyramids. 

The third epoch was that of Christianity, when knowl- 
edge and cultivation were spread among the common 
people, through the equalising influence of this religion. 
Mankind began to think less of the particular mode of 
sepulture and more of the state of the soul afler death, 
and felt, in a limited degree, the influence of those 
tender sentiments connected with the dead, which are 
most observable in a highly civilized people. Barbarous 
nations, it is true, venerate tlie tombs of thehr ancestors, 
but they manifest little of tliat feeling of posthumous^ 



p> ^-'-.- V ^ '•' 



.> ^, I 



\ « MOUNT AUBURX. 

^friendship and romantic yencration for the memory of 
jthe departed which are peculiar to modem Christian 
nations. It is a sentiment too spiritualized and poetic 
for any of the ancients, except tlie Greeks and Romans. 
It seems to have come up at tlio same time with tho 
sentiment which we call a love of nature, and is perhaps 
one of the legitimate fruits of Christianity. The dead 
> were buried in the churchyard, from the belief that, by 
such a mode of burial, tlicy secured tho salvation of tho 

^ toul when placed under the protection of the cross ; and 
they were laid in a grave, that they might sleep in tho 
bosom of nature, and under the sacred light of heaven. 

But Christianity has been very slow in performing its 
work. In the middle and feudal ages great expense was 
still hivished upon the tombs and monuments of kings, 
princes, and nobles. Hence tlie fourth epoch, or tliat of 
I rural cemeteries, is of recent date, and affords an example 
probably of the most expedient and benign method of 
disposing of the remains of tlTe dead. In the United 
States this mode of burial has been carried to the nearest 
perfection ; and though there is a proneness among our 

>. people to copy some of the senseless follies of the Paris- 
ians, in manifesting their respect for the dead, and some 
of the useless ambition of the English, in the erection 
of costly marble monuments, — notwithstanding these 
circumstances, rural burial is probably destined to be 
carried to perfection in beautiful simplici^, by some 
^ture examples in this country. 

When wo read of the tombs of the anciento, and 
consider their grandeur and their magnitude, we are 
prone to feel humiliated, because the monuments of Uiose 
early times so far suqiass our own in cost and magnifi- 
cence, and even in the genius that must have been 
nqmred to plan and design them. But we should cease

to humble ourselves by any such reflections, if we did 
but consider that, at a remoter period, extending far 
back into the ages of barbarism, they were still more 
costly and magnificent than in the middle ages ; and if 
we could look with a prophetic eye beyond the present, 
into that period of the future, when the human race shall 
have attained die i>erfcction of civilization, wo should 
probably witness a simplicity unsurpassc<l by anything 
tho world has ever seen, in connection with tlie finer 
works of art. 

[The Bunker Hill Monumcn^ which is a mere toy, in ^ 
comparison with similar works of ancient times, was yet 
too vast for the sujicrior intclHgenre of our people. Had 
it been delayed fifly years longer, it could not have been 
erected. (Tho people of New England arc fixie, ancP 
colossid architecture is tlie united work of dcf l>otism and 
slavery^/ As men advance in intelligence and freedom, 
they make art contribute to tlieir comfort, their con- 
venience, and their pleasures ; and the works of archi* 
tccture and sculpture will bo constantly growing less and 
less stupendous, the palaces of tlie wealthy and noble will 
be less costly, and tlie dwellings of the common people 
more comfortable and commodious, as the light of a more 
rational system of Christianity is shed abroad among men. 
Tombs can never again be so niagnifireiit as in the early 
ages, unless mankind should relapse into barbarism. Men 
will think more of nature and less of art, when providing, 
memorials and a resting place for the dcad.J 

The ancients built stupendous tombs, in which tho 
dead were piled up without regard to any feeling, except 
perhaps, tliat of rescuing them from the dust, as if by 
embalming and preserving tlieir bodies, they obtained for 
them the boon of immortality. Now we place tlie body 
in the grave ; we consign dust to dust ; wo restore the

remains of our friencb to the bosom of the cartb, and we 
make their mortal part a humble offering to nature, while 
we commend the immortal spirit to the God who gave it 
The ancients had less distinct ideas of the sours im- 
mortality, and cherished an inferior amount of tender 
sentiment in connection with the dead. They had no 
love of nature ; since the love of art, for the display of 
art, precedes in the human breast, this more fervent and 
poetic feeling. Hence their tombs, — many specimens 
of which exist on a small scale in eveiy burial ground 
at the present time, — were mostly revolting objects. It 
is now customary to bury the dead in graves, covered 
with the green turf r.nd the wild flowers of the field. 
Men will gradually learn to set less value upon art in 
this connection, and will think more of nature. They 
will learn tliat the only service we ought to render the 
dead is to secure their remains from desecration in the 
grave, and to provide a simple and durable monument, 
for the record of their virtues, and to serve as the means 
of identifying tlieir place of burial.

tliat in death they mny be surrounded by those objects 

which wore ngn^cable to them in their life-time, that tlio

they lie, and the birtls and insects make melody over

when wo are gone to our last repose, wo have no con- 

iciousncsa of our situation — there is something within 

the mind which intimates that the spirit may be hovering 

near, and may even in its heavenly state feel the benign ^ 

influcnco of nature that breathes around the phice where 

its mortal dust is deposited. 

We indulge tliis sentiment more deeply as it relates to 
the burial of our friends, than in anticipation of our own 
dcatJi and burial. Wo feel a serene satisfaction in know- 
ing that a beloved friend, whose ear can no longer hear ^ 
the words of life, is buried wider a green tree, beneath 
whose shade wo may resort, when we would offer to tho 
deed the tribute of our sorrow to our veneration. Tho 
trees, the flowers, the still waters, and tho green knd- 
seapo, allied as tliey are with the themes of poetry, with 
our ideas of hoaven, and with tlie hopes of our unmor-

tali^, soften our grief into a tender ntbkncholy, and quiet 
tlie anxieties of faith. The clouds that gather about the 
western sun shed tlie glow of heaven upon the gloom of 
the grave, and affect the mind with a deeper realiaition 
of tlio promises of religious hope. Every flower tliat 
springs up fifom the green turf, in its embossment of 
evergreen mosses, seems like a special messenger of con- 
solation ; and we cannot avoid tlie reflection tliat tlie re- 
mains of our friends repose more quietly in tlieso rural 
shades, and that tlieir spirits are blest by the same objects 
tliat tranquillize our sorrows. 

As wo stroll through the grounds, we read lessons 
which heaven, through nature, conveys to us in many a 
pleasing emblem of light and beawty. Tho winds rcpny 
sent tlie vicissitudes of life: but they inculcate the lesson 
that there is no adversity that is notVolIowed by the tran- 
quillity of a bettor (hiy. The flowers bud and bloom, and, 
in their vernal loveliness, represent tho morning of our 
days and the spring-time of our life ; but tliey perish, like 
our own corporeal frames, to indicate by their revival 
that new life, of which death is but the celestial dawning. 
The trees that spread their branches and extend their be- 
nevolent shade over tlie graves of our friends, are a man- 
ifestation of that unseen power tliat has assembled tlie 
departed spirits under his providential care. 
LThcro is not a more pleasing doctrine of religion than 
that faitli which views all material objects as tho repro- ^J 
sentations of something more beautiful and divine existing 
in the spiritual woridj To know all tliat is hereafter to 
be known would unfit the mind for the enjoyment of tlie 
pleasures we derive from studying the evidences of tilings 
unseen. A perfect certainty of future bliss must benumb 
tliat seal which arises from a consciousness of tlie neccs- 
■ity of exertion, in order to obtain the objecto of oar

believe, /jmaginationl presents us glimpses of divine 
troths, whidi reason will not allow us to believe witli tlio 
ibll assurance of a positive faith. Imagination affords us 
these gleams of light to cheer and encourage the ardor 
of hope; but|rca8onlsuggest8 doubts, lest in the full cer- 
tainty of celestial happiness, wo should renounce the 
grosser cares of this world, and surrender ourselves en- 
tirely to the future. The benevolence of the Deity docs 
not wholly conceal, nor does it fully unfold the most de- 
lightful realities of the heavenly world. 

Hence the different forms and aspects of nature are 
allowed by the Deity, to be tho- material representations 
of the blessings of another existence. Every object that 
is charming to our senses derives half its charm from its 
moral, religious, and emblematical signification. From 
these suggestions of divine things pi-ocvcd all the poetry, 
the beauty, and the romance of the material world ; and 
the reason why many persons have no passionate love of 
nature is, that tliey have never learned to interpret these 
emblems tliat appear on tho face of the eartli and tlie 
heavens. Tlie pleasures we derive from Uie verdure of 
the fields, the pyramidal forms of tlie trees, the blooming, 
the fading, and tlie lesurrection of flowers, are the pleas- 
ures of a religious and a poetical mind ; and there is not 
A beautiful object in nature that does not borrow its light 
and its loveliness from heaven. 

How would the gorgeous and varied tints and forms of 
the clouds fiide upon the imagination, if it perceived in 




them no similitude to tho conceptions we liave formed of 
celestial glory and beauty, or if tlicse objects never sug- 
grated a tliought beyond this mundane world. Tlie 
mind is enabled to extend its thoughts furtlier into infin- 
ity by the sight of these radiant hues of sunset, and to 
feel a rapture which is capable of being inspired by no 
other natural scones. All this proceeds from our habit of 
associating them with our ideas of the soul's immortality, 
with the infinite attributes of the Divine Being, and with 
our hopes of anotlicr and a brighter life. 

If an unbeliever derives a similar pleasure from tho 
same objects, he too is religious in tlie midst of his unbe- 
lief. Though his reason does not acknowledge a system 
of theology, he cherishes tlicse fond ideas in his mind as 
i»lcasing illusions to which ho yields a sort of poetic faith. 
The very uncertainty of religious truths lenders them 
more dear to our souls, as we cling witli greater affection 
to a friend who is absent, and whoso fate is involved in 
mystery. The doubtfulness of these points is necessary 
for our contentment with the unsatisfying realities of life 
— a contentment which is needful to the enjoyment of 
our existence. 

Through nature, in her myriad forms of beauty and 
sublimity, has tlie Deity benevolently given us intima- 
tions of these truths ; and the more we study their forms 
and aspects, the more vivid will bo tliese intimations, and 
tlie more devoted our fiiitli in tlie dim but pleasing assur- 
ances whidi tliey bring to our minds of the reality of 
what cannot bo known, until mortals have become im- 
mortal. It is while animated by tliese feelings tliat we 
delight to surround tlie tombs of our departed friends, 
witli all the beautiful objects of nature ; in tlie fields 
where the sods that cover tlieir graves are full of signifi- 
cant forms — the symbols of life, death and immortality;

beneath the bine sky, which is the emblem as well as the 
real image of infinity ; and beneath the clouds, which, 
under their ethereal banners, seem to open Uie gates of 
heaven to tliose who ore leaving this world. 

One day in the summer of 1850, as I was taking a 
^T- r '^ *>'>**T "^^^^^ >" ®"® ^^ ^^^ ™"^ villages, I saw a young 
woman, neatly but plainly attired, sitting npon a knoll, 
under a largo tupclo tree, that spread its branches over 
the widening of a small stream in the valley. She had 
evidently been weeping, and had dried her tears on seeing 
me approach. I made an apology for interrupting her, 
and then remarked that the little valley in which we had 
met was remarkaliy beautiful and almost enchanting. 
** Yes,** she replied, ** and it is particularly so to me, for 
here my sister, who died three years ago, used to come 
with me often, on plcoRant afternoons ; and here we sat, 
sometimes with a book and sometimes with our needle- 
work ; and here wo gathered a great number and variety 
of wild flowers, which she pressed with her own hand 
between papers, and gave them a name. Some of the 
names were of her own invention ; but I always call tlie 
flower by the name she gave it.** 

I inquired if her sister was buried here. ** She is not,** 
•he replied, ** but here I know, if her spirit dwells near, 
the would delight to have been buried ; and I have trans- 
planted many of these flowers upon her grave and around 
it, taking them up on a trowel with the sods, while tliey 
are in bloom, and they have seldom failed to come into 
blossom there the following seasons. I have often wiuhed 
•he was buried here, but in that cose, I should not enjoy 
ihe pleasure of transplanting tliese flowers on her grave, 
which is in a burial place not far distant.'* Do you thitik 
your sister is conscious of these offerings to her memory. 
^ I think 80 ; and this belief is the source of all my 



RURAL BURIAL. lO 

present happiness. It is this only that saves me from 
despair. I feel when looking over her preserved flowers, 
and when I am watching the buddmg and blossoming of 
tlie flowers npon her grave, tliat I am in her presence ; 
and it is Uiis reflection thnt solaces my grief. These 
flowers, and all tlie objects in tliis beautiful valley, are 
emblems to my mind of my sister's life in heaven ; and I 
think more of tlie flowers tliat spring up from her grave, 
tlian I should of the proudest monument that was ever 
carved out of marble.** 

Thus memory, as well as poetry and religious senti- 
ment, endears and hallows these natural scenes as the 
proper places for the repose of the dead. Our remem- 
brance of the incidents of their life is intimately associated 
with the grove, the hillside, the path by tlie river, and 
with other rural walks. In Uieir company have we be- 
come fiimiliar and delighted witli these scenes and objects. 
Tlie trees have a sacredness which is due to their alliance 
with the memory of our departed friends ; the flowers are 
the reflection of the smiles of those whom we loved. And 
when we come abroad under the open sky, surrounded by ' 
these mcinorinls of our friends, in the midst of tliese ma- 
terial forms of loveliness and beauty, and tliese emblems 
of our religious faitli and our trust in heaven, we do not 
turn away gloomy and desponding; but sit down with 
full assurances of meeting tliem again, when the evidence 
of divine trutli, which is only emblemined in nature, beams 
upon us in the full blan of celestial glory.

THE CHAPEL. 



Thb Cliapel was erected for the performance of burial 
senrices in those cases in which the state of the weather, 
or other circumstances connected with the funeral of the 
deceased, might render it necessary or conyenient It 
was designed also to afford a depository for statues and 
other works of sculpture which require protection from 
the weather. Tliis building is also of granite, and is 
situated on a conspicuous elevation, at the right of. 
Central Avenue. It is sixty feet by forty in its dimen- 
sions, and its decorations are in the pointed style of 
architecture. 

The windows are of colored glass, and in the head of 
the lai^ nave window is an emblematical device, con- 
sisting of a winged female figure reposing in sleep upon 
the clouds, and holding two'sleeping infants in her arms. 
It is designed to symbolize the tranquillity of death. 

The rose window in front contains a pauitcd emblem 
of immortality, represented by two chorube with an up- 
ward and prayerful look of devotion.

HISTORY OP MOUNT AUBURN. 

It appears tliat the earliest meeting assembled to con- 
Bidcr the project of a Rural Cemeteiy in the vicinity of 
Boston, was hold in November, 1825, at the house and 
bj the request of Dr. Jacob Bigelow. The individuals 
who were present on this occasion, besides Dr. Bigelow, 
were John Lowell, Geoi^ge Bond, William Sturgis, 
Thomas W. Ward, Samuel P. GanUner, John Tappan, 
and Natlian Hnle. The project met witli unanimous 
approval, and Messrs. Bond and Tappan were appointed 
a Committee to make inquiries, and report a suitable 
•pot of ground for tlie purpose. The Committee were 
unsuccessful in their inquiries; tlicy made no report of 
then: proceedings, nor was the subject actively revived 
bjr the above named persons. 

Tlie next movement was made in 1830, when Dr. 
Bigelow, — who seems to have remained firm in his 
original purpose, — having obtained from George W. 
Brimmer the offer of tlie grounds known as Sweet Au- 
bum^ for a public Cemetery, at the price of six tliousand 
dollars, communicated the fact to the other ofRcera of 
the Massachusetts Horticultural Society, — of which 
institation ho was at that time Corresponding Secre- 
taiy, — and engaged their co-operation in an earnest 
eflTort to accomplish the object of his wishes. A meeting 
of the members of that Society was held on the twenty- 
third of November, by invitation of Dr. Bigelow and 
John C. Gray, to discuss tlie project of a Cemetery, 
to be coimected with an ExperimenUU Garden of the 
Society. A C<Mninittee of the Society was appointed, 
coosistiiig of H. A. S. Dearborn, Jacob Bigelow, Ed-

ward Everett, George Bond, John C. Gray, Abbott 
Lawrence, and George W. Brimmer. 

These gentlemen called a more general meeting on 
the eighth of June, 1881, to consider the same subject. 
Joseph Story took the Cliair. and Edward Everett acted 
as Secretary. Great interest and entire unanimity were 
expressed in regard to tlie design of the meeting. It was 
also voted to purchase •• Sweet Auburn," provided one 
hundred subscribers could be obtained, at sixty dolhirs 
each. A Committee of twenty ^vas apjwinted to report 
on a general plan of proceedings proper to be adopted 
for effecting the objects of the meeting. The following 
are the names of the Committee : — Joseph Stoiy, 
Daniel Wel>stcr, H. A. S. Dearborn, Cliaries Lowell, 
Samuel Appleton, Jacob Bigelow, Edward Everett, 
George W. Brimmer, George Bond, A. H. Everest, 
Abbott I^wrencc, James T. Austin, Franklin Dexter, 
Joseph P. Bradlce, Charles Tappan, Charies P. Curtis, 
Zebedee Cook, Jr., John Pierpont, Lucius M. Sargent, 
and George W. Pratt. 

An eloquent Report on the general objects of the 
meeting was presented by tlie Chainnan of the pre- 
viously appointed Committee, H. A. S. Dearborn. 

Another meeting was held on the eleventh of June, 
1880, and heard the following Repfirt of tlio Committee 
of twenty ! — 

1. That it is expedient to purchase, for a Garden and 
Cemeteiy, a tract of land, commonly known by the name 
of Sweet Auburn, near the road leading from Cambridge 
to Watertown, containing about seventy-two acres, for 
the sum of six thousand dollars : provided tliis sum can 
be raised in Uie manner proposed in tlie second article of 
this Report. 



2. That a tabscription be opened for lots of ground 
in the said tract, containing not less than two hundred 
square feet each, at the price of sixty dollars for each 
lot, the subscription not to be binding until one hundred 
lots are subscribed for. 

8. That when a hundred or more lots are taken, tlie 
right of choice shall be disposed of at an auction, of 
which seasonable notice shall bo given to tlie subscribers. 

4. That those subscribers, who do not offer a premium 
for the right of choosing, shall have tlieir lots assigned to 
them by lot 

6. That tlie fee of tlie land shall be vested in the 
Massachusetts Horticultural Society, but that tlie use of 
the lots, agreeably to an Act o: tlie Legislature, respect- 
ing the same, shall bo secured to the subscribers, their 
heirs and assigns, forever. 

6. That the land devoted to the purpose of a Cemetery 
sluUl contain not less than forty acres. 

7. That every subscriber, upon paying for his lot, 
shall become a member, for, life, of the Massacliusctts 
Horticultural Society, without being subject to assess- 
ments. 

8. That a Garden and Cemetery Committee of nine 
persons shall be chosen annually, first by the subscribers, 

^ and af^rwards by the Horticultural Society, whose duty 
it shall be to cause the necessary surveys and allotments 
to be made, to assign a suitable tract of land for the Gar- 
den of the Society, and to direct all matters appertaining 
to the regulation of the Garden and Cemetery ; five at 
least of this Committee shall be persons having rights in 
the Cemetery. 

9. That the establishment, including tlie Garden and 
Cemetery, be called by a definite name, to be supplied 
by the Committee. 



The Society, on this occasion, accepted the Report, 
and autliorized the same Committee to proceed in the 
establishment of a Garden and Cemetery, in conformity 
to their Report. 

In June, 1831, the following Act of Incorporation was 
obtained from the Legislature : —

and thirty-one.

to the powers already conferred on Uiem, to dedicate and

hereafter to be purchased by tliem, as and for a Rural

Some account of Mount Auburn itself, as it existed 
at Uiis stage of its history, may with proprie^ be hero 
introduced. The tract of land which bears this name is 
situated on the southerly side of the main road leadiijg 
from Cambridge to Watcrtown, partly within the limits 
of botli those towns, and distant about four miles fipom 
Boston. Formeriy it was known by the name of Stone's 
Woods, the title to most of tlie land having remained 
in the family of Stones from an early period after the 
settlement of the country. Mr. Brimmer made purohase 
of tlie hill and port of the woodlands within a few yean, 
chiefly witli tlie view of preventing the destruction of tlie 
trees, and to his disinterested love of tlio beautiful in 
nature, may be attributed die preservation of tliis lovely 
s)iot The first purohase of the Society included between 
seventy and eighty acres, extending from the road neariy 
to the banks of Charles River. The Experimental Gar- 
den commenced by the Association was to have been 
upon that portion of the ground next to the road, and 
separated firom tlie Cemetery by a long watercourse, 
running between this tract and the interior wood-land. 
The latter is covered, tliroughout roost of its extent, 
s 



with a Tigtvrotu growth of forest trees, many of tlicm of 
Uuige siac, and comprising an anusunl variety of kinds. 
This tract is beautifully undulating in its surface, con- 
taining a number of bold eminences, steep acclivities, 
and deep shadowy valleys. A remarkable natural ridge 
with a level suriace runs tlirough tlio ground from 
aoutli-east to north-west, and has for many years been 
known as a secluded and favorite walk. Tlio principal 
eminence, called Mount Auburn in the plan, is one 
hundred and twcnty-fivo feet above the level of Charies 
Rjvor, ond commands from its summit one of the finest 
prospects which can be olitaincd in the environs of lios- 
ton. On ono side is the city in full view, connected at 
its extremities with Cliariestown and lloxbury. The 
•crpentino course of Charles lii%'er, with tlio cultivated 
hills and fields rising beyond it, 4ind having tlio Blue 
Hills of Milton in tlic distance, occupies another portion 
of the landscape. The village of .Cambridge, with tlie 
Yenerablo edifices of Harvard University, are-aituated 
about a mile to the eastward. On the north, at a very 
iniall distance. Fresh Pond appears, — a handsome sheet 
of water, finely diversified by its woody and irregular 
shores. Country seats and cottages, seen in various 
directkms, and tliose on the elevated land at Water- 
town, especially, add much to the picturesque effect of 
the scene. 

The grounds of the Cemetery were laid out with

THE MORAL INFLUENCE OP GRAVES. 

Tub melancholy pleasure which we derive from visit- 
- ing a place of banal is common to those who possess an 
ordinaij amount of cultivation. Tlicre are few persons so 
fiiTdoos as not to be made thoughtful, and few so heart- 
Jess as not to feel some emotions of humanity, after an 
boor's meditation among the tombs. The cause of the 
pleasore derived from this source, it would be difficult to 
explain, except on the supposition tliat melancholy, when 
gmtly excited, is an agreeable sentiment The grief we 
•offiar from the death of a friend, is for many days ex- 
tremely painful, and it is only our veneration for the dead 
that prevento our making a resolute effort to banish the 
•ol^ect of it from tlie mind. This painful grief is not 
-very lasting, except in extraordinary cases, or in minds 
pwdi^Hiied to insanity. As time wears away, it sub- 
rnks into a quiet state of Uie mind, wliich is tlie melan- 
choly of tlie poets, and a very different sentiment from 
that to which the same term is applied by medical writers. 
At this later period, the remembrance of the virtues of our 
departed friend, and of the many happy hours we have 
pMsed ti^gether, forms an agreeable retrospect, which is 
hallowed and made affecting by our subdued sorrow. 

Men in general are not prone to seek those objects that 
fofdbly suggest the idea of death ; but tlie contemplation j 
of the gravot of our fellow beings, produces a pensive state 
of the mind that overcomes our natural horror of death, / 
eqpedaUy when associated with certain fanciful unages 
emblematical of peace and immortality. We are more 
agreeably aflected in grounds of a simple and rustic ap- 
than in a highly ornamented scene, where the

artificial objects tJiat are pkced over tlie dead remind us 
only of the wealtli or the vanity of the living. People 
are attracted in multitudes to a burial place tliat is covered 
with gewgaws and expensive follies ; but they go there to 
gratify their curiosity, not to yield up tlieir hearts to medi- 
tation. The emblems of grief and of Uie rektions of 
time and eternity are picturesque and affecting ; those of 
vanity are simply diverting ; and when tlie works of vwi- 
ity are conspicuous among the tombs, the visitor, while 
examining the several objects which arc before him, for- 
gets the character of the place, and feels only tlmt idle 
interest that leads one jiassivcly along among the objecte 
of a museum. A cemetery is the last place in tlie world 
that ought to be made a scene of any such idle diversion. 

Neither ought these grounds to be made a place for 
general recreation. The idea of connecting tliem with a 
public garden was unwise, and were proved inexpedient. 
If our citiscns feel the want of a place for rural recrea- 
tion, where they can employ themselves in cheer^l 
festivities, and enjoy the beauties of nature, they should 
purchase a pleasant and extensive tract of pasture and 
woodland, and devote it to these purposes. But a place of 
burial should be consecrated to sorrow and meditation, 
and to that religion which offers consolation to tliose who 
are laden with grief. No merry-making or idle and 
thoughtless amusement should ever desecrate tliis spot, 
which has been made holy by the sad and solemn pur- 
pose to wliich it is devoted ; and tlie flowers and the 
landscape should bo made to smile and look beautiful 
around it, not to render it a scene of pleasure, but that it 
may seem more closely allied to heaven. 

To.tliese consecrated grounds we would resort as we 
attend service in the house o f^od, to indulge m serious 
meditation, and to ponder on tlioae themes which are

ir is in great measure our love of virtue, and our

We wish, as it were, to deify the spirits of the departed ;

Thus as time passes away, those who had brit few

8SPULCH&AL MONUMENTS OF THE MIDDLE AGES. 
(CoimxcD.) 

The eailiest tombs found in Great Britain, which can 
be considered as at all of an architectural character, are 
the stone coffins of the eleventh and twelfth centories. 
The oorers of these were at first simpler coped ; after- 
wards fireqnently ornamented with crosses of yarioos 
kindst and other deyices, and sometimes with inscrip- 
tions. Snbseqnentlj, thejr were sculptured with recum- 
bent figures in high relief; but still generally diminishing 
in width firom the head to the feet, to fit the coffins, of 
which thejr fiMrmed the lids. Manj of tlie figures of this 
period represent knights in armor, with their legs crossed. 
Tliese are supposed to have been either Templars, or 
mich as had joined, or rowed to join, in a crusade to the 
Holjr Land. These figures usual!/ had canopies, which 
were often richlj carved over the heads, supported on 
small shafb, which ran along each side of the efiigj, the 
whole worked in the same block of stone. This kind of 
tomb was sometimes placed beneath low arches or re- 
cesses, formed within the substance of the church wall, 
nsoall/ about seven feet in length, and not more than 
three high above the coffin, even in the centre ; these 
arches were at first semicircular or segmental at the top, 
afterwards obtusely pointed. They often remain when 
the figure, or brass, and perhaps the coffin itself, has long 
disappeared and been forgotten. On many tombs of the 
thirteenth century, there are plain pediment-shaped cano- 
pies over the heads of the recumbent effigies, the earliest 
of which contun a pointed, trefoliated arched recess. 
Towards the end of the century, these canopies became 
gndoally enriched with crockets, finials, and other archi- 



MONUMSNTB OF THE MIDDLE AOE8. 



48 



The most of the monuments of tlie middle ages were 
erected soon afbr the death of the persons whom they 
commemorate ; but in some instances, the parties buried 
in them, prepared them during their lifetime. These 
were firequently the wcaltliy ecclesiastics. There are 
but few existing monuments which are earlier than the 
twelfUi century. In the reign of Edward I., the tombs 
of persons of rank began to be ornamented on the sides 
with armorial bearings, and small sculptured statues, 
within pedimental canopied recesses ; and firom these we 
may progressively trace the peculiar minutiss and en- 
richments of every s^lo of ecclesiastical architecture, np 
to the Reformation. 

Altar, or table tombs, called by Lekmd ** high tombs," 
with recumbent effigies, were common during the whole 
of the fouirteenth century. These sometimes appear 
beneath splendid pyramidal canopies, and sometimes 
beneath flat testoons. In the early part of the same 
century, the custom prevailed of inlaying fiat stones with 
brasses, and monumental inscriptions frequently occur. 
The sides of these tombs are sometimes relieved with 
niches, surmounted with decorated pediments, each con- 
taining a small sculptured figure ; sometimes with arched 
panels filled with tracery. Other tombs, about tho same 
period, but more frequently in tho fifteenth century, were 
decorated along the sides with large, square, panelled 
compartments, richly foliated or quatrefoiled, and con- 
taining shields. 

Many of the tombs of the fifteenth and sixteenth centu- 
ries appear beneath arched recesses, fixed in, or project- 
ing firom the wall, and inclosing the tomb on three sides ; 
these were constructed so as to form canopies, which are 
often of the most elaborate and costly workmanship. 
These canopies were sometimes of eldlxtrately carved 



MOUNT AUBURN. 

wood, and 8ometiiiie8 the altar tomb of an earlier date 
was aft a later period inclosed within a screen of open 
work, with a groined stone canopy, and an upper story 
of wood, forming a mortuary chapel or cliantry. 

In the early part of the sixteentli century, the monu- 
ments were generally of a similar character to those of 
the preceding age ; but alabaster slabs, with figures on 
them, cut m outline, were frequently used. The altar 
tombi, with figures in niches, carved in bold relief, wcro 
also frequently of alabaster, which was extensively 
quarried in Derbyshire. The Italian syle of funereal 
architecture soon came into general use, and was often 
mixed with the old style. In the two following centuries 
every sort of borbarism wu introduced on funereal mon- 
wnents; hot the ancient style lingered longer in some 
pbces than in others. 

The fashion of representing on tombs the cfRgy of the 
deceased graven on a plate of brass, which was imbedded 
in melted pitch, and firmly fastened down by rivets 
leaded into a slab of Purbeck marble, appears to havo 
been adopted about the middle of the thirteenth century. 
These memorials, where circumstances permitted, were 
often elevated upon altar tombs ; but more commonly 
they are fbnnd on slabs which form part of the pavement 
of churches ; and it is not improbable that this kind of 
memorial was generally adopted for convenience, that the 
area of the church, and especially the choir, might not 
be encumbered, as was tlio case when effigies in relief 
were introduced. 

The lepolchral brass, in its original and perfect stnto, 
was a work rich and beautiful m decoration. It is by 
careAd examination sufficiently evident that the incised 
lines were filled up with some black resinous substance ; 
the armorial decorations, and in elaborate specimens, the 



m^wm 



MONUMBNTS OF THE MIDDLE AOBB. 

whole field or background, which was cut out by tlie 
chisel or scorper, were filled up with mastic or coarse 
enamel of various colors, so as to set off the elegant 
tracery of tabernacle work wliich forms the principal 
feature of ornament. 

In England it was usual, with few exceptions, to inlay 
on tlie hce of the slab, the figure and tlie di£Eerent orna- 
ments, arms, and inscriptions, graven on detached plates 
in distinct cavities, which seem to have been termed cose- 
ments ; so that tlie polished slab was left as tlio field or 
background. On the continent, possibly, in consequence 
of the brass plate being more readily obtained, the fashion 
was different One large, unbroken surface of metal was 
obtained, formed of a number of plates, soldered together, 
and upon tliis surface all pnrts that were not occupied 
by the figure, or tlie shrine work around it, were en- 
riched by elaborate diapering, usually armorial, the design 
being sometimes arranged loacnge-wise. Brasses of tliis 
more costly kind exist in England, but all hitherto 
observed are of Flemish workmanship. It may be 
remarked that the barbarous custom of using old grave- 
stones, when tliey happened to be convenient, is to be 
found in early times as well as late ; and monumental 
brasses do not appear to have been exempt from the same 
fate, as older inscriptions may firequently be found on 
the back of them. The author of Piers Plowman's Creed 
taxes tlie friars with this practice, that they might make 
room for other tombs, and get moro fees. The &shion 
of sepulchral. brasses continued for moro tlian four cen- 
•turies. A remarkaWo specimen of tlio latest works of tliis 
description is tlie fuU-sizcd effigy of Samuel Harswult, 
Archbishop of York, at Chigwell, Essex. He died hi 
1681. On the continent the engraving of sepulchral 
brasses has, in later times, been resumed ; a noble brasi'

MOUKT AUBURN. 



of full liie haying been engraved in 1887, at Cologne, as 
the memorial of the late Archbishop. In England, like- 
wise* a rerival of the art has recently taken place, and 
■erend brasses of good character have been executed. 

It is to the continent tliat wo must turn to seek the 
origin of sepulchral brasses, and it maj be traced with 
little hesitation to the early enamelled works in France, 
diiefly produced at Limoges. This art was introduced, 
most probablj, hj Oriental or Byzantine artists, and as 
earijr as the twelfth century, the ** opus de Limogia *' 
was celebrated in southern Europe. Of tho larger works 
of thk kind scarcely any spcdnions have escaped. The 
costly tombs, with effigies of metal enamelled, which, 
prior to the revolution, were seen in many cathedrals 
in France, were all converted into cannon and copper 



** Most of the earlier brasses are lost, and it is lamentar 
hie to reflect upon the causes of their destruction, having 
been produced either by puritanical affectation or by theft. 
The value of the metal was ajcmptation to sacrilegious 
robbery ; the brasses were with little difficulty torn up, 
and in timei of disturbance these were removed, the 
places they occupied being now alone remaining to us, 
and giving simply the outline of their figure :

** How short-sighted is man I Tlie means adopted by 
him to secure posthumous reputation, by the employment 
of the valuable metals, in the construction of tombs and

copidi^ of others, and occasion thoir removal. Thus

PLOW£RS AROUND GRATES. 

Flowbm have been regarded in all ages as the most 
appropriate ornaments of a burial place, and have always 
been strewed upon the bier and grave of a friend, as tlie 
most significant offerings of affection. So many tender 
recollections of flowers are linked with tlie memoiy of 
a beloved fncnd who has departed from us, that when 
we see them springing up from a new-made grave, the 
image of the dead is brought vividly before the mind, 
and while our sorrows are revived, they are soothed and 
tranquillized. Flowers are particularly suggestive of the 
interesting events in the life of a litUe child. So often 
has a cluster of wild flowers been the occasion of some 
of its happiest moments, that the smiles of the living 
child, and tlieir forms and colors are closely allied ; and 
when we see them adorning the grave of the little slum- 
berer, we feel that its spirit must be more blessed in 
beaven. The mother who has lost a child is prompted 
by this sentiment to strew flowers on the grave of the 
baried one, that its young spirit may see the evidence 
of the mother's love and the mother's grief, and derive 
from them the same pleasure which they afforded in its 
lifetime. But- when a flower comes up spontaneously 
on the little grassy mound, it seems to the disconsolate 
nother like a tribute of afibction from the dead to the 
living* 

It if so common to eulogize flowers as the acoonn 
panimento of graves ^ embiematicai aa^.they are of our 
unmortality, and of tlioso virtues which prepare lis for

ANCIENT INTERMENTS IN GREAT BRITAIN. 
Fmmi PmiAjiT*! Tom ur WAia. 

Sefvlciiral tumuli aro Tcry frequent in tlio village 
of Llanannon. I was present at the opening of one, 
composed of loose stones and earth, covered with a layer 
of soil about two feet tliick ; and over tliat a coat of 
verdant turf. In the course of our search were dis- 
covered, towards the middle of the tumulus, several 
urns made of sun-burnt clay, of a reddish color on tlie 
outside, bUck within, being stained with the ashes they 
contained. Each was placed with the mouth downwards, 
on a flat stone; above each was another, to preserve 
them from being broken by the weight above. Mixed 
with the loose stones were numerous fragments of bones. 
These had escaped the effects of the Are of the funeral 
pile, and were deposited about the urns, which contained 
tlie residuum of the corps, tliat had been reduced to 
pure ashes. This custom appears to have been of very 
high antiquity; it was in use with the most polished 
nations, — with the Greeks and the Romans, as well as 
with tlie most barbarous. The ancient Germans prac- 
tiaod this rite, as appears from Tacitus. The Druids 
observed the same, with the wild addition of whatsoever 
was in use in this life, under tlie notion that they would 
be wanted by tlie deceased in the world below ; and in 
coiifiniiati<m of this, arms and many singular things of 
tuiknown use, aro to this day discovered beneath the 
place of ancient sepulture. The remote Sarmatae, and 
•11 the Scandinavian nations, agreed in burning the dead ; 
and the Danes distinguished by tliis, and the different 
throe soreral epochs : —

The first, which was the same with tliat in question, 
was called Roisold and Brcndctiide, or the age of

was placed entire, with all the ornaments which graced

be buried with him, from the pcnuasion tliat the soul

Th(v third age was called Christcndons-old, when tho

Norwich observes, ** abhorred this species of obsequies ; 
and though tliey stickt not to give their bodies to be

unto ashes, but unto dust again." 

From tlie remarks of these able writers we may leam

therefore fell first into disuse with the Britons; for it

I beg leave to add the account given by Virgil of the 
funerd rites of Pallas. We find in it many ceremonies 
that were used by the northern nations. Animals of 
diffiuent species were burned or deposited witli the body. 
The spoils of war, and weapons of various kinds, were 
plarcd on the pile ; the bones and ashes were placed 
tc^ther ; and a heap of earth or a tumulus flung over 
them. Some of each of these circumstances are con- 
tinually discovered in our barrows. Horns, and other 
relics of quadrupeds, weapons of brass and of stone, — 
all phuxd under the very same sort of tombs as are 
described by Virgil and Homer. Perhaps the other 
ceremonies were not omitted ; but we have no record 
that will warrant us to assert that they were in all 
reqiects similar. 

The Tuscan chief and Trojan prince command 
To ruse the funeral structures on the strand ; 
Then to the piles, as ancient rites ordain, 
Theup friends convey the relics of the slain. 
From the black flames the sullen vapors rise. 
And smoke in curling volumea to the skies.

The foot tlmce compass the high, bhudng pyres ; 
Thrice move the horse in circles round the fires. 
Tlieir tears, as loud tliey howl at every round. 
Dim their bright arms, and trickle to the ground. 
A peal of groans succeeds ; and heaven rebounds 
To the mixed cries, and trumpets martial sounds. 
Some in the flames the wheels and bridles throw, 
Tlie swords and helmets of the vanquished foe ; 
Some the known shields their brethren bore in vain. 
And unsuccessful javelins of tlie slain. 
Now round the piles, the bellowing oxen bled. 
And bristly swine ; in honor of the dead. 
The fields they drove, the fleecy flocks they slew. 
And on the greedy flames the victims threw.

CURIOUS RITES OP DIFFERENT NATIONS. 

Muieovian Funerals, — In Muscovy, when a inan dies, 
. his friends and relations immedintcly nsscmblc, and scat 
tliemselvcs in a circle around tho ' corpse, and ask the 
dead tho following questions : — *• Why have you died? 
Is it hecauso your commercial afiiiirs went hadly? 
Or was it because you could not obtain the accom- 
plislimcnt of your desires? Was your wife deficient 
in youth and beauty? Or has she been faithless to 
her obligations?'* They then rise and quit tho house. 
AVhcn they carry the body to be buried, it is covered, 
and conveyed on a bier to the brink of the intended 
grave ; the covering is then withdrawn, tho priest reads 
some prayers, the company kiss the dead, and retire. 
These ceremonies finished, the priest places between the 
fingers of the dead man a piece of paper, signed by tho 
patriarch confessor, purporting his having been a good 
Christian. This, they suppose, serves as a possport to the 
other world ; and from its certifying the goodness of the 
deceased, St. Peter, wjien he sees it, will'open to him 
the gates of eternal life. The letter given, the corpse 
if removed, and placed in the grave, witli tlie face to- 
wards the east 

InUreHing Cuttom. — A custom that was once preva- 
lent in Spain is deserving of notice. When any one 
dies, the relations, friends, and neighbors, carry to the 
furvivors, at meal times, for three daj-s, one or more 
plates of food, under the idea that tho grief they feel 
will not allow them tor think on nutriment. Some por- 
ous also accompany these dishes, in order to offer con- 
•olation to the family.

«« In Louisiana, there is a custom," says an anonymous 
female writer, *• which I believe is peculiar to the place — 
that of bearing a young bride to tlie grave in her wed- 
ding attire ; and there is something in such a rite which 
must deeply affect the most heartless. Some months 
since I stood by tlie bier of a young bride. She had 
been lovely in life, and there was a sculpture-like beauty 
in the marble face before me, — a solemn loveliness in 
the rigid countenance, — which spoke more forcibly to my 
heart, than it could have done when animated by the 
changing expressions of life, for it told of a far, far land. 
I had expected to see her arrayed in the simple robe 
with which we dress the dead, and to feel alone in the 
presence of the mighty conqueror who sways his sceptre 
over all. 

•* I was ushered into, a lai^je gloomy room ; long wax 
candles shed a flickering light through the apartment, 
and the deep and smothered sobs of the mourners were 
all that broke ite silence. I went.witli others to look 
upon tlie dead, and I sliall never forget my surprise, 
or the strange and singular emotions which tliat view 
excited. Stretched upon her bier lay the young bride, 
who a little while before had been animated with life 
and joy. The rich and costly rolxs which she had worn 
upon lier wedding day, enveloped her figure, and her 
pale hands, witli tlieir glittering jewels, were clasped upon 
her breast ; while the orange blossoms shone among her 
dark locks, and the veil, widi its gaudy folds, fell beside 
her. It was a lovely, yet a mournful sight, to see her 
arrayed in tlie habiliments of joy, and lying in her still, 
calm beauty, in tlie embrace of death — the bride of the^ 
gra ve. Tlie gloom and silence of the room, tlie rigid

It is A task, reqairing, perhaps, one of the highest 
efforts of genius, to combine the works of nature and 
art, wherever there is a multiplicity of objects, in such 
a manner as to produce in the combination a harmonious 
and beautiful unity. There is no place wh ere tliis exer* 
ciw of judgment, pn_ the. part of tlie artist, is s o ncces» 
sary, as in a cemetery. Sculpture might be carried to 
perfection in tlicse grounds, and be made to enhance tlio 
desired effect, if the artists would always govern them- 
selves by certain general principles. An obelisk rising 
up in tlie centre of a grove of trees, is an object which 
no rules of taste would condemn. Build a highly orna- 
mented fence around it, and yon introduce a trifling 
formality, that injures the unity of expression, in the 
same manner as if you were to plant a border of box, or 
a circular hedgerow, about each of tlie trees. The most 
venerable ruins have always sprung from tlie finest styles 
of architecture. A chaste style of sculpture is necessarf 
on the same principle, to produce tliat union of simplicity 
and grandeur, without which a rural cemetery becomes 
like a mere magnificent toy-shop. 

In laying out a rural cemetery, two points desenre 
consideration : — first, the general design of the whole ; 
second, the porticuhur design of individual objects. 
The general design is to impress the visitor witli a pro- 
found religious sentiment, and a feeling of devout con- 
templation. Tliis can only be promoted by causing tlie 
grounds to wear an expression of 9olemnUtf and grandeur ; 
and these may be said to constitute the two gcnend 
effects which are to bo studied. 

The particular design of individual objects is to per- 
petuate tlie memory of the dead ; and this is promoted 
by constructing tlie monuments and tlieir apimrtcnances 
in a beautiful, simple, and appropriate style. Btonlif^

If we wonld heighten the religions influence of the 
objects in a cemetery, we must lay out the grounds and 
build the monuments and their appurtenances in such a 
style as to solemnixo the feelings of the spectator on his 
immediate entrance. This solemnity of clmractcr per- 
vading tho place, ])repares the mind to feel the emotion 
of sublimity, which is one of the most exalted of our re- 
ligious sentiments. It is produced by the contemplation 
of etcnn'ty, and of the immensity of the universe. It is 
increased by all that is mpterious in the doctrines of re- 
ligion, in the future condition of the soul, and the infinite 
attributes of the Deity. It is not tlie religious alone who 
are susceptible of such emotions, or who can appreciate 
the benign influence of such contemplations ; for this 
susceptibility is an innate faculty ofjhe human mind. 
Were all the accompaniments of a cemetery so designed 
as to harmonize with these feelings, every one must be 
aware of the superior imprcssiveness of its scenery, 
compared with its present influence. Keitlier wouhl 
tliese circumstances scr^'e to diminish any other effect 
wo might wish to attain. Indeed, when the mind is tlius 
elevated by solemn enthusiasm, it is peculiarly suscepti- 
ble of all tender and virtuous sentiments ; and while tlie 
soul is tlius exalted to heaven, it feels a deeper and more 
tender interest in tho state of the dead, and the welfare 
of the living. 

grandeur J which is the second general effect to be 
studied, is not identical with solemnity ; but the latter is 
greatly promoted by the former, and the same objects 
are ofien equally productive of each of these sentiments. 
A grand style of architecture in the interior of a church 
adds greatly to its solemnity, and heightens the in- 
tensity of all our intcllcctiwl, emotions. It cannot 
be denied that the feeling of cheerfulness is exalted al- 



rfr\:

PSIKCIPLBS OP ART. 



77 



to a degree of tnuuport by an expression of gran- 
ind brilliancy, in a ball where a party is assembled, 
jojrftil or festive occasion The merry music of the 
, and the solemn harmony of the Christian serrice 
[ually enhanced by tlie majestic appearance of tlie 
I which they are performed. Anything that exalte 
iod prepares it to feel more mtensely any emotion 

the scenes or performances may be designed to 
ce. There is no feeling or sentiment which it is 
Ue to cherish, tlmt would not be heightened by a 
il expression of grandeur amid the scenes and ob- 
i A rural cemetery. 

\ question next arises, by what means these two 
il effecte may be promoted. They come equally 
he style of laying out the grounds, and from Uiat 

objecte included witliin them. It is erident that 
nred expression cannot be produced by tombs that - 
rered or surrounded by ridiculous or meretricious 
ishments. But there bre, unquestionably, certain 
tioDs which serve the particular purpose of beauti- 
be monumente, without injuring the unity of the 

scene. Such are all those emblematic designs 
ce characterized by simplicity, which is not the 
I barrenness, or monotony. Simple omamente aro 
I do not counteract the effect intended to be pro- 
bj the principal object, either by exciting opposite 
mtB, or creating confusion in the mind. An illus- 
of this principle may be drawn from music, 
accompanimente of a simple air are necessary to 
oe to ite expression. If tliese are of such a char- 
I to give the air a stOl greater prominonce in the 
I attention, they are compatible with simplicity. 
wbOe thoy produce no matter what pleasing effect, 
vwn the air, eitHer by not harmoniznig with the 



1 



theme, or by exciting in the mind a different emotion 
from tliat which should bo awakened by tlie wr, the 
accompanimente are in bad taste, and the opposite of 
simplicity. The omamente around a grave should bo 
emblematical or suggestive of some pleasing moral or 
religious truth ; but like true wit, they should be obvious 
and intelligible, and, like a correct stylo of architecture, 
free from those trifles that serve to divert the mind from 
the principal design. 

Nothing so greatly interferes with an expression of 
grandeur as a multitude of small parte. It is on this 
account that a grove of trees has more majesty when di- 
vested of ite undergrowth, than when thickly interwoven 
with shrubbery. In a cemetery, this multiplicity of small 
parte cannot be entirely avoided ; but it may be remedied, 
in a measure, by tlie exclusion of fences, flower beds, 
all excess of monumental stones, useless ornaments, and 
a superfluous amount of shrubbery. By avoiding thisf 
defect, we should promote both that;imity jmdhiurmsiij. 
which are so necessary to produce a deep influence on -^ 
the mind. In order to secure all these important olgects, i 
the grounds ought to be under the supervision of a board 
of trustees, who should exclude evoything that would 
derange the harmony of the grounds, by the introductkni 
of a false ornament, like an accidental bathos, in a poo- 
sage of pathetic or sublime eloquence.

Heu I will rest, upon this hillsido fiiir, 

And muse upon the scenes within these grounds, 

Where towering oaks keep out the mid-day glare, 

From whoso hroad tops como forth sweet mellow sounds, 

Like funeral chants o*er these sepulchral mounds. 

I am alone, and I would wish it so ; 

For witli high interest the spot abounds. 

And while mj thooglitf witli solemn fervor glow, 

I would a lesson learn, ere to the world I go. 

It is the hush of Autumn's solemn tide ; 

Far in the west the Sun his course hath spent ; 

Across the heavens the wild clouds swiftly ride. 

While scarce a ray to light my patli is lent: 

*T is true I come no lost friend to lament. 

Yet I 're a tear to give to those who mourn ; 

And even now my rising sighs are spent. 

As towards yon grave witli musing steps I turn. 

Where virtue lies enshrined beneath the voiceless urn. 

I love the spot, for bright in memory's page, 
Ccnnes up the day, when bidding books farewell. 
With buoyant steps I came to hear the sage 
Whote silver voice arose from yonder dell, * 
While crowds sat breatliless as his accents fell.

It was a lovely day, the morning sun 

Walked m rich splendor up the ambient sky. 

And when his western goal was nearly won. 

Each haunt of this faur wood glowed with his brilliancy. 

But ah, how changed I Uiis lovely spot then soemod 
Like opening paradise to my young heart ; 
And nature here in rich luxuriance teemed. 
Where monuments now rise of vying art : 
O I why should pride in tliis still spot have part t 
'Rather let nature in her wildness live, 
And o*er all scenes her living hues impart. 
From whence the soul heaven's blessing may derive. 
And feel its lagging powers again in life revive. 

The evening sluides are fast assembling round. 

And to his airy seat each songster hies, 

Wlule all is hushed throughout this hallowed ground. 

Save where from yonder mart low sounds arise. 

That lull the ear like gentle melodies. 

And now I bid these scenes a sad fiirewell. 

Where many a noble breast in quiet lies : 

Ere I again shall come, ah I who can tell 

How many a breathing form may seek its narrow cell.

eithnr of a theatrical nature, or an entire departure from 
the solemn purpose to which the building has been erected. 

There is a philosophy in this matter, and it is the phil- 
osophy of the heart. Similar feelings apply to the mon-

placed, and classical and heatlien personifications and

Wheh we are walking in a rural cemetery, it would 
be instructive to note the different emotions with which 
we contemplate the various objects that greet our sight. 
We view this monument without any feeling at all ; the 
next may prompt us to'say to ourselves "How much 
money has been lavished here ! Tlie builder must have 
been eitlier a very rich or a very foolish man." We 
pass on to another, in which we behold an indescribable 
quality, tliat affects us with a sensation of the ludicrous, 
or with, perhaps, a feeling of indignation. Tlicre is 
something that savors of vanity in the style of it, some- 
thing that suggests an idea of the sinister character of 
the proprietor for whom the artist designed it. We 
may be unable to point out the particular defecto of the 
monument, or the decorations that produce our dislike ; 
•s in an ugly iace we cannot always determine what are 
the features that render it disagreeable. The same emo- 
tions are probably fclt by almost every intelligent per- 
son who views the monument. Yet this same monument 
may be greatly admired and celebrated, because it may 
be veiy costly, as a foolish book is sometimes admired 
because tlie author has made a fortune by tlie sale of it. 
There is only now and then an eccentric individual who 
would venture to utter his real opinion concerning the 
book or the monument Others are cither diffident of 
thdr own judgment, or are afraid to differ from what they 
■uppose to be puMie opinion. All despise it, and all pre- 
tend to admire it Thus is the whole community ofVcn 
governed by a delusion, and a bad taste appears to pre- 
vail, because no man dares to deviate from a certain 
■tmdard which falsely represento public sentiment

We next arrive at a monument that suddenly awakes all 
our sympathies, that causes' us to feel an interest both in 
tlie history of the*ono who lies below, and in the mourner 
who erected the stone. We do not think of its artistical 
merits, nor of the wealth,, nor the taste, nor of tlio char- 
acter of the owner. We tliink only of the dead as a 8ul>. 
ject of regret, and of the mourners as objects of sympathy ; 
or something in ito appearance awakens a tender sentiment 
of melancholy, or fills the mind with a deep religious 
solemnity. All this is tlie effect of the style of the mon- 
ument It produces the effect which ought always to bo 
studied, but one which is very difficult to be produced. 
Genius is required for such a design ; that looks to the' 
moral influence of the work upon the mind, and not to 
mere display of art 

Splendor neutralircs the influence of every scene that 
is intended to produce the sentiment of melancholy. Ar- 
tificial decorations, even when consisting only of flowers, 
if not arranged with extraordinary skill, mar that simple 
beauty which is the most interesting in a grave, and rob 
it of its sacred influence. Yet there are serious and de- 
lightful emotions which are ofVcn excited by an appro- 
priate and expressive monument The stone tliat marks 
tlio resting-place of one who wos beloved and innocent in 
life, becomes a symbol of all that is divine in our natures. 
From tho same source comes tlio pleasure we feel from 
the perusal of an epitoph, enimmerating tlie virtues of 
some humble friend of humanity. 

Our ancestors marked tlie places where Uieir dead re-

stone, whose sombre hues tliey believed to bo in harmony 
with tho solemn character of tlie grounds. Some of tlieir 
inscriptions were ludicrous attempts to bo patlietic, and 
tlie emblems engraved upon their headstones, are often

In designing the objects for a rural cemetery, it should

which are in unison with the character of the grounds. 
^j^^PlBki^lj'I^Sb^J^ may gratify his own vanity in a

ANCIENT FUNEREAL PRACTICES. 
Bklbotbd akd CoHPiun. 

N0TWITH8TANDIN0 tlio melancholy gloom which tlie 
ancients cast over all tlicir ideas of death and the grare, 
botli in tlioir moral and poetical writings, tlicjr appear in 
reality to have endeavored as much as possible to lighten 
those impressions, and place at a distance those dark 
phantoms of the imagination. Accordingly, the deep 
and solemn sadness attending our burials; the black 
shades of yews and cypresses ; tlie dreary chamel house 
and vaulted sepulchres ; tlio terrific appendages of mould- 
ering bones and winding sheets ; 

** Hie knell, the ■hrood, the mattoek, mad tlM gnTt, 
The <leep, daaip tmU, the darkaen, end the wwm," 

which, from custom, form so great a part of the horror 
wo feel at tlie thoughts of death, were to them unknown. 
The corpse consumed by funereal fires, and the ashes en- 
closed in urns, and deposited in tlie earth, presented no 
ofibnsivc object or idea. Besides, to dissipate the sorrow 
of the living, or, perhaps, with a desire to gratify the 
spirit of the dead, wines were poured and flowers scat- 
tered over the grave. These offices were the grateful 
tributes of love and veneration. The manes of the 
deceased, still wandering about the place of interment, 
might, perhaps, partake of the libation and enjoy the 
odor; at least his memory would be honored, and his 
spirit delighted. 

Whatever may have been the original purpose of these 
ceremonies, we find repeated allusions to them in the 
poets. Anacreon mentions the rose as being particularly 
gratefuL The tomb of Acliilles was adorned with the

We will offer the reader a few examples of the monu-

found some of tlie best and most affecting epigrams that

is by the poetess Erinne : — 

I mark the spot where Delia's ashes lie ; 

Whoe'er tliou art that passes silent by 

This simple column, graced by many a tear. 

Call the fierce monarch of tlie shades severe. 

These mystic ornaments too plainly show 

Tlie cruel flite of her who lies below. 

With the same torch that Hymen gladly led 

The timid virgin to her nuptial bed. 

Her weeping husband lit the funeral pyre. 

And saw the dreary flames of death aspire. 

Tliou, too, Oh, Hymen t bad'st tlie jocund lay, 

That hailed thy ibstive season, die away, 

Changed for tlie siglis of love, and groans of deep dismay. 

It is worih while to observe the allusion in tliis epigram 
to another custom of tlie Greeks, who frequently adorned 
the tomb with some symbols indicative of tlie peculiar 
circumstances attending tlie death of the deceased. 

The affecting incident of an unfortunate woman dymg 
in a foreign land, surrounded by strangers, is preserved

The next epigram, which, in the original, is addressed

The truth is, that in their thoughts and reflections on 
death, mankind liave ever had in view some idea of a 
consciousness that remains and lingers round the **plea9i 
ing, anxious " solicitudes and scenes of life. They have 
ever imagined to tlicmselves a spirit after death, that 
busied itself in protecting the feme and character of 
their lives, that was yet sensible to slights or honors paid 
to the grosser and earthy parts. 



THB SEPULCHRES AT THEBES. 

BOOKISOIUII. 

The sepulchres of Memphis were placed in splendid 
pyramids, wholly above ground, and built upon a rock ; 
but at Thebes they are subterranean entirely. They are 
situated in a spot about a mile from the walls of the dty, 
which is, from this appropriation, called the ^ Valley of 
Death.** Passuig up a narrow ravine, barren and deso- 
kte, in which not a blade of verdure is to be seen, the 
visitor beholds on the left hand the apertures that lead to 
the tombs. When I was there, there were twen^-foor 
of them open ; but since then, Belaoni has di s covered 
10 



Kjjp 



110 



llOUKT AUBURN. 



tweatj more. There maj very probably be one hundred 
more yet to be developed by tlie researches of future 
tmvdlers. Such of them as are accessible, resemble each 
other so closely in all respects, tliat a description of one 
may serre for the whole. 

The excayations are carried tlirough tlio solid rock, 
deep mto the bowels of the mountain. Some of tlioM 
rocks are of limestone, others a species of sandstone. The 
entrance of the sepulchre is an aperture of twenty by 
twelve or fifteen feet This leads to a perforation or 
tonnel, the walls of wliich are made to incline inward for 
the sake of greater strength, and which extends in none 
of them less than one, and in some as much as two miles. 
At the termination of this tunnel we come to the Hall of 
Death. It is wider than tlie passage leading to it, and 
contains an altar and a sarcophagus. In some this latter 
is made of sienite granite ; in others of basalt ; in others 
of a species of green basalt ; and in otliers again, the 
sarcophagus is of alabaster. Delzoni brought one of these 
away, and carried it to London. 

These extensive perforations through solid rock must 
liaTe cost an amazing amount of labor. Each of them 
is equal in sise to the far-famed tunnel under tlie Tlmmes« 
.the cost of whkh was nearly tlireo millions of dollars. 
Admitting that the multitudes employed in tliese excava- 
tions were slaves, and wrought without wages, still they 
must have been clothed and fed, so that the cost must 
have been very great But the mere hewing and re- 
moval of the stone was but half the task. As the rock 
left a rough surface, the whole had to be covered over 
with stucco, an mch and a half or two inches in depth, 
like the composition called chunam^ employed for artificial 
hearths or floors. On this substance were sculptured 
battles, triumphs, sacrifices, and all those represenUtions, 



TUE sEPUIiCHRES AT THEDE8. 



Ill 



which form tlie usual subjects of the Egyptian chisel ; 
and when tlie carving was finished, the whole surface was 
painted, —so profuse of labor, time and cost, were those 
who executed these astonishing works I 

The colors used for this purpose, after the lapse d 
twenty-five centuries, remain to this day as fresh and 
bright as if laid on but yesterday. This is a phenomenon 
which forcibly excites the wonder of all beholders. It is 
probably to be attributed to some singularity of the color- 
ing matter employed. It was the opinion of Sir Hum- 
phrey Davy, that the colors must all Imve been vitrified, 
so as to give tlicm tlie durability of glass or enamel ; bq^ 
he could not conceive wlmt medium had been employed 
to unite and blend them. 

While the walls are thus covered with painted sculp- 

I } ture, the ceiling is painted of a deep azure, and adorned 

J with numerous representations of stars, tliese having their 

\ forms sunk somewhat below the surfiice of the ceiling 

around tlicm, and covered with silver leaf. Tlius as tlie 
astonished visitor is examining tliese chambers by torch 
light, he beholds above his head an artificial heaven, 
while on every side ho is surrounded by tens of thousands 
of figures and devices of various forms, until he feels 
bew'ildercd and overcome by the strangely impressive 
scene, and the thrill of mingled awe and wonder seises 
every fibre, botli of the body and mind. To get some 
idea of the multitudes of figures on the walls, I took the 
trouble to count those which occupied a space two feet 

V wide and twenty feet in height, extending from tlie floor 

to Uie ceiling, and I found them to be two hundred and 
fifty-four. Tlicy all seemed to liave a meaning ; and no 
doubt many parts of tliem could have been read and in- 
terpreted by one who liad tlio requisite skill ; so tliat 

i probably the contents of ten thousand volumes may still 



vinbl^ recorded on the walls of the«e ancient 
Uamhtf ^ere to be preferred for the instruction of. figea 
and generations yet unborn. There are ^fkyfiuch cham- 
bers acceasible at present, and the number increase as 
inyestigation proceeds. Who may conjecture the flood of 
historical light which may yet be poured on the latter age 
of the worldf should the key to these mysteiiouB repre- 
ieutations ever be dlscorered 7

OLD GBAYETAROS. 



Thbez ftre few places which we yisit with more interest 
than those old banal grounds, so frequent in our early 
settlements, and in which the dust of our ancestors is hud. 
• We obserre in their appearance a charming simplicity, 
that attracts the attention of all visitors, enlists their 
sympathies with the dead, and excites a tender veneration 
for their memory. No exhibition of pride awakens en- 
Tioos feeling*, or causes emotions which are not in harmony 
with the sacredness of the grounds. The mosses of age 
have softened the glitter of sculptured art, and given the 
sober tint of antiquit)' to the monuments, some of which 
have lost their uprig it position, and bend like humble 
penitents before the divine altar. Many of the works of 
the chisel, that might originally have eroblcmized the 
pride of the builder, have been chastened and subdued 
by the agency of time, and wear the look of the most 
humble designs. When we jvandcr among tliose vener- 
able scenes, we are impressed with all those tender and 
religioas emotions, to which the burial place ought always 
to minister. We stroll among tlie most interesting of 
ruins, and while we look upon the old headstones, with 
their quaint emblems and devices, our reverence for the 
dead is exalted to a quiet enthusiasm, and all our thoughts 
are raised to heaven. 
~ There is some danger that the custom of building 
ahowy monuments in our modem cemeteries may prevent 
our posterity from visiting them with the same feelings 
, which we experience when contemplating those of our 
predeoesson. The simplicity that pervades these old 
is absent from the new, which are filled with 
of livuig pride. Time, it is true, will cast a

veil over the glitter of all this ambition, and cause the 
most showy objects to wear a more humble appearance. 
But the original and unaffected simplicity of our old 
graveyards must ever be wanting in tliose, in which the 
vanity of the living has sought gratification over thw 
resting places of the dead. 

Our ancestors entertained certain gloomy views of re- ^lU 
ligion and of our state hereafter, which a more enlight- ^^T'

inscriptions that appear on the headstones in their places 
of burial, are indicative of tlicir peculiar feith, and do not

distingnished their lives, and of that humility in which

The flowers, emIxMomed in the mosses which for many 
years have been accumulating upon the soil, spring up 
with a singular charm around these old graves. The 
spade has not profimed their venerable mounds since tlie 
earth was first thrown up to receive die mortal remains 
that rest beneath them. But many a pious mourner has 
bedewed them with tear8,jmd watched with sad pleasure 
the first violet of spring that appeared on the recent sods, 
and the latest evening primrose that lingered upon them 
m the melancholy days of autumn. And after the sor- 
rowing had ceased, and the mourners likewise were 
gathered onto the dust, the flowers still performed their 
sacred ofiice around the old forgotten graves, as if some 
unseen spirit still watched over them and cherished the 
neglected sod. 

In proportion to the age of these grounds, and as time 
has spread a velvet surface of mosses upon them, is their 
apparent alliance wiih nature. The trees that bend over 
the tombs have extended their roots into the dilapidated 
^i, and afanost obliterated them ; and the old head- 
that lean firom theur original erect position, are 



^ 



decorated with lichens of various colors, causing them to 
resemble the rocks in the solitary pastures. A profusion 
of wild shrubbery has diffused itself in irregular masses 
among the graves, and in some places, while the wild 
roses blossom upon the turf, the vine of the American ivy 
has wreathed itself luxuriantly about Uie monuments. 
The most humble shrubs are commonly the most mter- 
esting ornaments of these mdosures, because, like humble 
graves and humble cottages, they are more closely allied 
with poetry. When I witness these scenes, I am not 
affected with sadness. Though tlie living no longer 
mourn over the dead who lie there, Nature has received 
them into her own bosom, and with maternal fondness 
has wreathed the loveliest garlands of beau^ around 
their graves. 

In an ancient burial ground, it is pleasing to mark this 
providence of Nature ; to note the tender care with 
which she clothes the baldness of the crumbling hillock 
with herbage and flowers, and tlie old monuments with 
plants that do not perish like the flowers, but live on, year 
afWr year, and symbolize, in their ever durable tints, that 
immortality which has become the portion of those who 
have left their mortal remains to the faithful trust of 
nature and of the tomb. The most reverential efibrts of 
human hands could not have wreathed about their tombs 
so many objects that endear the spot to our affcctkms. 
Vainly would the gardener's art attempt to rear in their 
places groups of more interesting plants, or hallow their 
graves by more affecting remembrancers. 

There are no persons of ordinary education and refine- 
ment, who do not linger with rational delight in an old 
graveyard ; and I have observed that they do so, not 
from a mere desire to read the quaint inscriptions upon 
nhe monuments, but evidently from a proneness to mdulge
