those feelings of reverence for their tncestors which the 
scenes inspire. Men who have but little reflection or 
sensilMlity, are by these objects converted for a time into 
poets and philosophers. The skeptic cannot enter here, 
'without a lemporaiy revival of those charming religious 
influences tliat filled and inspired his youthful mind. 
Above all, do persons of religious fooling and poetic imag- 
I ination love to ponder among these scenes. Nature and 
art and religion have united to form here a combination 
I of objects tending to humanize those who have forgotten 
\ their love for their fellow-beings, and to raise the thoughts 
of the virtuous still ferther above the world. 

Notwithstanding the venerable and interesting charac- 
ter of these old cemeteries, there is a disposition among 
those who would sweep away every tiling with the besom 
'^^'^ improvement,'* to remove tliem, to make room for 
.< land speculations. Every old graveyard, tliough right 
in the midst of a village, should be preserved, not to be 
used for new graves, but set apart as hallowed ground. 
. The trees that stand there have formed a grove, which 
ought to be as sacred as any that were in ancient days 
consecrated to philosophy. Let tliem never be disturbed, 
that the ground may be used for ordinary purposes. Let 
them be cherislied as memorials of the simple habits of " 
our ancestors, who, though often condemned for their 
disregard for the ideal and the beautiful, never wandered 
in search of the vanities that characterize the present 
tvmes. It is hardly probable that we sliall escape the 
•itire of posterity, as our predecessors have escaped our 
own. Though no living mourners are seen to weep in 
these grounds, which have long ceased to be a place of 
sorrow ; yet they who go tliere, while pondering among 
the graves of their ancestors, may be persuaded to imitate 
their uiidatentatious virtues, and learn that wisdom which 
nay be read in their humble biography. 



THB OLD BURYINChOSOUin). 



119 



The old headstone that marks the grave of an ancient 
patriot, has no less value in our sight tlian if it was a . 
costly marble obelisk. It is a venerable ruin as dear to 
a patriotic breast, as the proudest monument of our mil- t 
itaiy glory. Underneath that stone lies tlie dust of one _ 
whose intellect assisted in modelling our republic, and 
whoso courage, undaunted by the threaU of ^rranU and 
the marshalling of inimical hosts, wrought our freedom 
and established our independence. The name of many a 
hero, and patriot, and martyr, is recorded upon these 
leaning monuments. Let us preserve them with religious 
care ; let nature overshadow them with trees, and let the 
wild-flowers of all seasons attract the young and the old 
thitlier for meditation, that, while lingering there, they 
may learn that wisdom which too many have forgotten, — 
the wisdom of a humble life, which is the only safeguard 
of virtue, and die only bulwark of liberty.

In many parts of Franco, but more especially in the

In the twelfUi and tliirtccnth centuries, sepulchral 
chapels, or else hollow columns, were often erected in

or hntems, which, by night, cast tlieir rays on all sur-

which the deceased might be exixiscd to view, laid in 
slate, or cared for as necessity might require before his 
interment; or here might be celebrated the office for the 
dead, and other usages, of which the memoiy is lost. 
They had, for the most part, the circular form, which 
was that of the Holy Sepulchre at Jerusalem.

GRAVES OF CHILDHEN. 

There is nothing of m melancholy descriptlan more in* 
tere^tlng or picturesque than the littlo grassy mound that 
marks tbo gmre of an Infant. It takes hold of the IbcUngs 
more sensibly than other grave*, and impfessed upon the 
mind the reflection that the little occupant had not lived the 
allotted period of human life, but w&s cut down prema- 
turely like a budding flower, before it had opened its eye» 
to the rays of ttie morning. On beholding it, we feel a 
tender sympathy, as for one who has been dcpriv*ed of 
joys that were prepared for hia tiiilUon^ It calls to mind^ 
the innocence of the cliild, its playfulness, ItThopefuhieBSf 
and its occasional Borrows f and the little mound becomet 
exp revive of many afTecting images of hope and dlnap* 
pointnient, of matcnial love, and its early bereavement. 
We think of the stem disease thfft deprived tlie young 
child of its life, of sufferings which it could not apeak, of 
maternal tears shed over its cmdle, and of the despair that 
accompanied its burial, so that a perfect poem could 
hardly be more suggestive than this little fbound. 

No other graves by their dimensions indicatp„th c ggn 
of the occupant) and its d i minutivencss becomes, tliere- 
fore, a part of its interestinjT diameter. I f one has ever 
lost an infant child, or an infant brother or sister, how 
vividly is the remembrance of its life awakened by the 
sight of one of these hillocks I It is a beautiful emhle- 
xnatic picture, in which the history of the child is related 
by the turfs, the flowers^ and tlie headstone, each bring* 
ing to light some interesting event in its life, or some 
pleasing trait in its budding afFectiona. No feelings but 
tliose of love and of sorrow, — no hate^ nor envy, nor 
jealousy, can be associated with such a grave ; and If

MOUMT AUBURN. 



angds erer come from heaven to linger aboat the scenes 
of earth, and to administer consolation to disinterested 
grie^ they most delight to watch over these little graves, 
to receive the sighs that are breathed over them, and to 
poor the halm of heaven into the hearts of the mourners. 

When cliildren die, the grief wo feel is tliat of affliction 
•lone, unalloyed with any selfishness or pride ; the foun- 
tain of that sorrow is as pure as the dews of morning that 
glitter upon mossy turf. If we have no connection with 
the departed, we feel a deep sympatliy with those who 
m afflicted, as sincere and unaifticted mourners. But 
the mother who has lost a child in ito tender years, as 
some one* has poetically remarked, is never without an 
Snfent. The other members of the young group attain 
the fblnesf of their adult years, and they are no longer 
children i and the remembrance of them, as such, has 
been obliterated by her intercourse with tlicm ui later 
life. She seldom thinks of them as children : but the 
little one that departed, in its infancy or childhood, re- 
mains always bright in the.memoiy of its parent She 
never forgeti its countenance, its motions, its smiles, or 
any of the interesting features of its character. All the 
incidenta of its short life are embalmed in her memory, 
and remain there with an ever«nduring affection and 
▼eneration. 

After the death of a beloved child, it may bo truly 
•aid, that there is always an angel in the house, iden- 
tified with all the scenes and incidents of the past, and 
hallowing those few years, during which it tarried on 
aarth, as a period peculiarly sacred to memory. The 
bereaved mother feels ever afterwards that there is a 
laneti^ about her dwelling, which she perceives in no 
other place exoept the house of God, and the room in

which she witnessed its dying sighs, is always from ihat 
moment consecrated to a£^tion and to sorrow. When 
the poignancy of recent grief has been softened into a 
melancholy and quiet remembrance, there is a perpetual 
fountain of liappinoss in tlie recollection of the lost one, 
whoso sacred image is associated with every scone and 
object with which it was familiar. Everything that was 
prized by the departed, has become sacred in the eyes of 
the parent ; and the places it frequented are illumined 
with tlie light of tlie afinsction she bore it, and of the 
smiles which were retumod. 

A similar but more melancholy light beams from the 
grave of the little sliunberer! tlie light that surrounded 
its dcatli-bed, and which was irradiated from heaven. It 
carries us back to tlie time when tlie dead n'as living ; 
and while it revives the sorrow tliat attended its death 
and burial, it awakens a crowd of cherished memories 
which are esitential to the liappipcss of a true mourner. 
It is like the light of tlie glowing sunset, which 
awakens a sad thought of tlie pleasures of tlie past day 
that can never return, while its radiant and melancholy 
beuuis glow prophetically with the assurance of another 
morn, which will be ushered by the same celestial hues. 
Then do we (eel tliat the purest joys of the soul aro not 
those which aro experienced in the bright sunshine of 
our day ; but tliat tlie melancholy tliat accoinimnies our 
roflcctions, when we tliink of past joys and departed 
friends, purifies and exalts our liappiness, and is blended 
witli something tliat seems bom of heaven. 

Tlie sight of tlie grave of an infent is affecting, even 
to arranger, who seldom beholds one without tlie re- 
vival of ablest of affecting remembrances. He thinks of 
bereaved affection, of innocence suffering on the bed of 
sickness, of a soul that is lost to the world, and of par 
12» 



rental grief that most endure forever. It is on the bosom 
of tnch a grave, that a little wild flower meekly rising 
from the green turf, has a charm beyond all the devices 
of art, and a significance that leads the mind to a closer 
communion with nature and the Dei^. If the spirit of 
the departed could communicate with the living, how 
deUg^itfiil would be the messages conveyed by this little 
flower, with its meek-eyed representation of innocence, 
and its emblematical expression of immortality. 

When we look upon the graves of children, and reflect 
upon their unseasonable death, we cannot avoid the be- 
lief that there must be some bright reversion of their iate 
in reserve for their spirits, and that they who were pex^ 
mitted to live but a fow brief years or days, and then 
returned to dust, must receive a blessed recompense in 
lieaven. It may be that they are earlier in their com- 
mencement of a new and happier life ; and thus the 
cruel^ of death, which deprived them of the joys of the 
world, may be in realify but the kindness of Providence, 
in opening to them prematurely tiie gates of immortality. 

It is ft conunon belief of Christians that some good 
arises out of eveiy affliction. This is not merely a re- 
ligions, but a philosophic sentiment. If I had not lost an 
infiunt brother in my youth, nor met with any similar 
^afflictioD', I am confident that I should have remained, 
during all that period of lifo, a stranger to some of the 
purest affections that flowed from this fountain of sorrow. 
With that mfiuit brother, though his death was followed 
■hf the most profound grief, are associated some deep 
tiioii^ts that would perhaps have remained latent through 
Kfo, untfl called forth by the sorrows of after years. 
They might never have taken root in the mind. I 
eoold not, at to early an age, have experienced those 
niptorooa risions that flow from our ponderings on whence 



the departed spirit had flown. All that imagmed worid 
of bliss that lies far off in the foture, which we call 
heaven, and believe to be allied with the beauties and 
sublimities of an etherial landscape, is made more vivid 
to the mind by the death of a near friend, during the 
religious and imaginative period of youth, especially if 
the lost one be an infant, who is the image of perfect 
innocence, and the fit inhabitant of a world of peace. 

Hence one can never behold the closed eyes, the f^^,^ 
pallid form, and the serene countenance of a dead child, tlvt. 
without being keenly reminded of all his most pleasing ^^-^ 
dreams of heaven. Wc think of a pure spirit released at .^^tt 
an early period from the struggling life of this worid, to 
join the company of angels in some more blissful sphere ; 
and we feel that death, which alone can confer immortal 
powers upon the soul of a mortal being, is all the change 
required to transform an infent into a winged cherub. 
The same thoughts are awakened by .the sight of an in- 
fant's grave. The flowers that cluster round this dimin- 
utive hillock, always seem brighter and holier than those 
we find in tlie open field, or by the roadside. 

I shall never cease to regret, therefore, the present 
custom of levelling the ground above the graves, and of 
leaving no rising mounds in our modem cemeteries, to 
denote the spot where the mortal remains are entombed. 
There is a picturesque charm about these funereal mounds, 
which no design in marble can supply in the place of 
them. Even the sculptured figure of the child reposmg 
in a niche in the monument, would not be more expres- 
sive, nor awaken more romantic images, nor invest the 
place with more sacredness, than the little hillock that 
measures the length of the coffin underneath the sods.

the pathos of elcgiae strain ; and whilst the magnificent

Not wanting in solemn pomp, in gorgeous ceremonial, 
in mystic and awful incantation, but yet reeking witli 
human sacrifice and unhallowed rite, was the religion of 
our ancestors in Britain, before tlie " tidings of great 
joy '* had reached our shores — ere ^ the beautiful foot ** 
of tlie MESSENOEa had alighted on the Uood-stauied

The learning and wisdom of the Druids have been

lay hold of tlie imagination in a cultivated mind, much to

religious solemnities. The deep, vast and solemn groves,

huge altar, stones, near each of which stood the atten-

When a person of more consequence died, his horse

lus of his mother.

finom an iron torques, or a silver or gold bracdet, to an 
ivory hook or crystal ball. 

But happy are we to turn from these slight though

period when the Day-star finom on high beamed over the 
earth, and the mild rays of Christiati hope penetrated 
the darkness and gloom, which had hitherto shrouded the 
borden of the grave.

EPITAPHS AND INSCRIPTIONS. 

- — n^;> 

Evert ponon of intelligeiice and BensibOitj is alive to 
the beauties of a brie( simple, and appropriate epitaph 
which excites a reverence for the dead, and awakens an 
interest in the events of his lile. When we enconnter a 
headstone without an epitaph, it seems like a book with a 
mere title page, while Uie leaves that follow are blank. It 
is an indispensable appendage to a monument, and we 
turn from one that is without it as from a work of sculp- 
ture that is unfinished. The propriety of this tribute to 
the dead is universally admitted ; and it is not, therefore, 
a useless task to endeavor to define the principles bjr 
which the composition of it should be governed ; for if 
one tliat is appropriate and well written, is pleasing to the 
most indifferent reader, one that is awkward, high- 
sounding or exaggerated, is ludicrous and demeaning to 
the character of the subject 

There are some epitaphs that relate particularly to the 
dead, and are commonly panegyrical ; otliers that make 
no direct allusion to the dead, but aim merely to convey 
a pleasing sentiment or an instructive moral. The 
former are the most difficult work for the writer ; be- 
cause it requires great discrimination, in el^ac compo- 
sition, to avoid the extreme of panegyric, or to. present, 
in a few words, the most appropriate thoughts and images, 
and to select tlioso points which would produce the most 
vivid effect upon the mind of the reader. If one is extrav- 
agant in his praises of the dead, the reader is sceptical of 
the truth of those praises ; if the epitaph be long, it will 
not be read ; and though it were brief, the points selected 
may not be those which would produce the most fovor* 
able impression. 



162 



MOUNT AUBURN. 



It has been the custom among writen of epitaphs to 
aim at antithesis; to express pointed thoughts in ap- 
posite wofds and phrases. This is the surest method of 
clothing a commonplace thought, or a trite image, with 
the appearance of originality ; but Ais style of writing 
it too artificial to seem to flow from the heart. When 
the composition is evidently studied, it loses its charm 
lor the reader ; though he may know at the same time, 
that the most pleasing simplicity is often tlie result of 
great art and elaboration. When one reflects that the 
living friend could write noUiing of the deceased except 
a pointed epigram, he is prone to imagine that, as there 
was nothing in his character to be praised, he was com- 
memorated only by a witticism. Sincere praise is often 
exaggerated, but never pointed and rhetorical. 

•* The difficulty," says Dr. Johnson, " in writing epi- 
taphs, is to give a particular and appropriate praise. 
This, however, is not always to be performed ; for the 
gmter part of mankind have little that distinguUhes 
them from others equally good or bad ; and, therefore, 
nothing can be «ud of them, which may not, with equal 
propriety, be applied to a thousand more. It is, indeed, 
no great panegyric, that tliere is inclosed in this tomb, 
one who was bom in one year, and who died in another ; 
yet many useful and amiable lives have been spent, which 
leave little materials for any other memorial. These, 
however, are not the proper^ subjects of poetry ; and 
whenever friendship or any other motive oUiges a poet 
to write on such a subject, he must be forgiven if he 
sometimes wanders in generalities, and utters the same 
praises over different tombs. 

The scantiness of human praises can scarcely be made 
more af^iaient, than by remarking how often Pope has, 
in the few epitaphs which he composed, found it neces- 



El'ITAPHS AND IN8CKIPTION3. 



163 



sary to borrow from himself. The fourteen epitaphs 
which he has written, comprise about one bundled and 
forty lines, in which there are more repetitions than wili 
easily be found in all the rest of his works." 

But the evil arising from the repetition of a thought, 
which has been frequently expressed in other oomposH 
tions of the same kind, lias been greatly exaggerated ; 
and there are many ideas that would be very appropriate, 
which are not contained in the compositions of Pope, 
who fell into the error of aiming to be pointed and an- 
tithetical, and to end his pieces in a climax. Hence, 
there are many natural and appropriate thoughts which 
he was obliged to reject, because they could not be woven 
into the pointed style of his compositions. Among the 
inscriptions to bo found in our country graveyards, are 
many that are preferable to any epigrammatic verses, 
which are sadly wanting in simplicity and pathos. Any 
man*8 life may afford a lesson to others ; and if that life 
was a virtuous fone, a few words announcing this feet, 
expressed neither in rhyme nor metaphor, may prodnce 
a deep impression upon the mind of the reader. 

" He lived in peace, because he was just ; »' 
** He died in hope, because he wai a Christian.*' 

Tliose lines convey no fulsome panegyric, and yet no 
higher praise could be bestowed upon one in so few 
words. They contain a two-fold moral, showmg tlie 
advantage of justice to secure a life of peace, and of a 
belief in Christianity, to die with a hope of Heaven. 

A part of the difficulty, attending the composition of 
epitaplis, arises from the effort of the writer, to express 
ideas and images which are not obvious, without con- 
sidering that this effort, if it be apparent, spoils all their 



164 



MOVinr AUBURN. 



cflfoct. It is not tlM highest praise tliat is most exaggenp 
tedy for lugh praise expressed in pUun and simple terms, 
if it produces conviction in the mind of the reader, gives 
rise to no invidious feeluigs. A smaller amount of eulogy 
coovejed in high^unding language, betrays the wish of 
the writer to raise his subject to an undue importance, 
and fiuls in producing conviction, because it excites our 
incrednli^. An epitoph should always contain more 
eulogy than is apparent, like a strong and even light that 
illuminates a room without danling the eyes. 

It has been customary, at certain times, to omit any 
inscripdon upon the tomb, except the name and age of 
the dec«ii^?d, and perhaps some other indispensable rec- 
ords. This neglect probably originated in a conscious- 
ness of the abuse which has been made of epitaplis ; their 
extravagance in some instances, and their triteness or 
absoidity hi others. These evils were tliooght to bo 
avoided by onutting the epitaph entirely. But if one 
olgects to the panegyrical epitaph, he might use the 
other form, in which a sentiment or a moral is merely

the character or history of the subject. Such is that 
oommon,bnt most appropriate and delightful sentiment, 
which has lost nothing by repetition, and is often in- 
scribed upon ihe tombstone of a little child : — 

<« Not lost, but gone before." 

There is stni another form of epitaph in which the 
person commemorated is represented as speaking. These 
difierent forms of inscription seem to give variety to the 
expression of the same ideas ; and as one form is not 
absolutely preferable to another, the writer should be. 
goTomed in his choice by his own taste. Of the last

XPITAPH8 AHD IllSCBimOlll. 



ICT 



with an epigram or a diswrtation. A sentimeiit ooiiTejned 
in language aimple enough to be intelligible to all« ba ni ihea 
the sospicion that the writer is endeavoring unjostl/ to 
exalt the dead above his real merits. The epita|^ should 
be simple, that all may onderstand it ; obvionSf that it 
may require no studj ; brief, that all maj read it ; mod* 
erate, that it may be credited ; poetical, that it may lay 
hold of the imagination ; cheerful, that it may reconcile us 
to our inevitable fitte ; religious, that it may inq[»iFe the 
hope of anew life. 

An epitaph is of no value, if it does not obtain the 
fiuth and the sympathy of the general reader. For thii 
end it should give proof of the writer's own deep feeling 
and sincerity. He must address the reader, therefore, as 
a humble firknd of the departed, and not as a sermonist or 
a censor. He must be serious and solemn, but not 
gloomy; believing and hopeful, but not extravagantly 
elated. His kmentations must be hesMfelt, bat not too 
painfully wrought; for the reader, though he loves to 
sympathise, does not wish to be afflicted. We sympathin 
more easily with sorrow that is sincere without despon- 
dency ; for we wish to see a probability that the mourner 
will obtain relief and a renewal of happiness, as we are do* 
lighted with the promise of morning that gleams through 
the darkness of night An epitaph should make no pa- 
rade of one*s lamentations, any more than of the virtues 
of the subject. As a silent tear flowing down the dieek 
of an unquestionable mourner, excites more sympathy 
than boisterous wailing, so does one line of tender an- 
guish afiect the sensibili^ of the reader more deeply than 
a long paragraph of earnest complaint. 

A sepulchral monument is no place for' wit or for 
satire. We may be excited to mirth by a humorous 
epitaph upon a gravestone ; but it interrupts the flow of

tender inelanclioly wliicK one la disponed to rherish in hw 
tneditationa among the tombs. It disqunliiics the mind 
to r^!eive congenial impressions, and docs not avert 
g]oomy reflcctiona with tlie same power oa the hopeful 
utterance of religious faith. Satire, which is always 
more or less malignant, ought to find no place here* 
Anything like malice or contempt towards our fellow 
being*, should never be exhibited in these sacre^i inclos- 
^ uies. The sight of tJie graves of our fellow men brings 
<t forciblj to mind the reflection that we are all travelling 
the same road ; and here we should unite in mutual 
trust and forbearance ; and if we have lessons to impart 
to the living in the lines which we carve upon the monu- 
ments of the dead^ let them be conveyed in tlie simple 
language of love, Let the graveyard be a schoql of re^ 
ligion and virtue, not a place for the wit of the ci>)giiim* 
matist or the sneers of the misanthrope* 

Death must bo mentioned as our inevitable fate, and as 
tlie occasion of sorrow ; but not as tlie cause of despon- 
dency, or the dostntyer of hope. The tomb should be 
invested with those circumstances that will shed light on 
the gloom of the grave ; and nothing serves more eifec- 
tually to diffuse this cheerfulnewt around it, than a 
poetical and hopeful inscription that j)oints to a world 
beyond this mortal sphere. 

The individual commemorated is to he presented to the 
reader aa one who has not lived in voinf nor died with- 
out hope ; and the claims upon the rcadcr^s interest and 
Aympathy should be based on his ordinary, not extraor^ 
dinary deserts. The first idea commands our sympathy, 
tlie second excites our incredulity. If the subject has 
pei-formed certain noble and heroic acts, it is better to 
name the acta, and let tliem praise him, than to follow 
them witli ejttravagant laudation. To say that one died in

of cnltiyated nature. Nothing, however, requires more 
taste, more discrimination of character, circumstance, and 
place, than the attempt to decorate in this manner.

pointed contriver, become an object of ridicule. The

FUN£fi£AL EMBLEMS AND S£Y1CE3, 

TuEEB are many tmtlia Aol explained by phUosopliy^ 
nor demonstrable by rea^oni wMcli may be lUoEtrated 
in A pleasing manner by eraUema, Science does not 
tt^cb us all we wisb to know, and imagination oflcn 
suggests a truth which is too deeply involved in mystery, 
to be clearly comprebendcd or fully believed. Religion 
is half buried in obscurity, and reveals doctrines which 
are inexplicable, aa heaven itaelf is invisible and the spir- 
itual world incapable of being located. Many ideas, 
connected with the state of our existence hereafter, belong 
to the same mysterious circle of truths. These ideas an) 
pleasingly iUustraled by emblems, which contoio intima* 
tlons, not demonstrations of truth, and afford glimpses of 
light which has never yet fully irradiated the buman 
mind. It is for these reasons that emblems are so gener- 
ally employed to convey to the mind an image of the 
souIV condition in the future world, and to impress it 
with a belief of tilings which are only dimly seen hy the 
eye of faith. 

An[einblei^m(iy be defined a visible image, or a picture 
that suggests to the mind the idea of seme abstract truth. 
It is indeed a pictured allegory or parable* In tJio East, 
emblems are still freely used in profane as well as in sacred 
things. Among the Persians fire is the embl^n of their 
Deity, expressing by the some image his power and his 
beneficence — Iieat being tlie source of all life, and having 
power to destroy all created forms of matter* The ser- 
pent with its tall in its mouth is an Egyptian .emblem of 
eternity ; but modem taste revolts at its pueriUty, and it 
really affords no idea of infinity, such as might be repre-

The study of emblems » closely connected with that 
of monumental sculptureni inasmuch as ttiey are the foun- 
dation of all those devices which are used to decorate a 
tombstone. A pleasing device is to the artist what a 
pleasing metaphor is to a poet. Indeed, a sculp tor ha» 
no other way of expressing his ideas upon marble, than ' 
b)f means of emblems, unless he gives the real image of a 
thing. The head with wings, that appears so often on the 
upper part of the head stones^ in our old graveyards, is 
an interesting device which was probably derived from 
tliat of the winged globe. The head is intended to rep- 
resent the soul, and the wings the image of its flight*. 
These and other devices, which were so generally em- 
ployed by the Puritans^ are vastly more poetical than 
those which are seen on the monuments of noble and 
royal families in Great Britain. On the latter are repre- 
sented tlie armorial bearings of the &mily and various

MOUBNIKG CUSTOMS. 
Bt Mm. Bion. 

Hardly more direnified are the nations who people 
the earth, than are the cnstonu and ohservances used bj 
them to signalize the arrival of the commonest of dl 
visitors, thoagh most awfbl of all guests, the ** black 
veiled king of the dead." The Jews of old rent their 
garments and sprinkled dust on their heads, a piactioe 
followed to this day in Abyssinia. The practicii of 
tearing the garments is, we are told, commuted hj the 
Jews of these economical days into carefully cutting 
away a small, and probably a perfectly insignificant por- 
tion thereof. They bottled their tears also, a custom 
reCerred to in the 66th Psalm; and that this practice was 
•customary with the Greeks and Romans, the number of 
lachrymatories, or tear bottles, found among their sepul- 
chral remains, sufficiently testifies. 

A late writer has p<mited out the analogy between a 
mourning custom of the Australian savages of to-day, 
and of the ancient Hebrews, vis., the cutting or scratch- 
ing the fiice with the nails, tearing the flesh between the 
eyes, and otherwise maiming the person, as is the custom 
of the female aborigines of Australia on the death of a 
relative. Hence the warning in Holy Writ— **Te 
shall not make any cuttings in your flesh (or the dead, 
nor print any marks upon you." Lev. six. 28. 

Ilie ^cup of consolation " referred to in Scripture, 
and the ** broad of mourning," sometimes called also the 
'* bread of bitterness," wero the refreshments always 
among the Jews, supplied by friends to the bereaved 
person on his rotum from the funeral — - in its origin a 
most kind and hospitaUe relief to the bereaved femily. 



£00 



MODKT AtTBURF. 



The Jew* cherish^ their grief in every way j liiey in- 
vited it ; they pampered it ; they took dl pains to recall 
the poignancy of tlieir afflirtion* They ate their food 
seated on the ground and without shoes. For three days 
Ihcy atroTe not to repress their tears. For seven daya 
people came morning and evening to weep with them. 
At the end of seven dftys the mourner might attend the 
synagogue \ but thirty days must elapse ere he was al- 
lowed to bathe, or to dress his beard. 

In many countries the term of mourning was fixed by 
law. The JewBi as we have seen, mourned thirty days j 
the Lacedaemonians but eleven j the Egyptians from forty 
to seventy days. Komulus fixed a widow^s mourning at 
ten months^ the length of his year. The Imperial Code not 
only ordained a yearns mourning, bat. declared the widow 
infamous if she married within that period. The time of 
mourning is fixed by law in China, three years being the 
period required for a parcnti 

The Jewish fnsliion ofjhrowing ashes on the head, 
beating the breast and tearing the flesh with the nails, 
was, on occaiions of peculiar concernment, adopted by the 
Greeks. But in addition to the funeral feasts, wliich 
among Greeks and Romans soon ceajscfl to wear an entirely 
lugubrious aspect, they enlivened their melancholy with 
games and funeral processions. Tliese entertainments 
among the Greeks consisted chiefly of horse-races, where 
garlands of parsley were awarded to the victors. The 
Roman g^mes were processions, and the very character^ 
istic entertainment of the mortal strife of gladiators and 
the funeral pile. These funeral games were abolished by 
the Emperor Claudius. 

A custom prevailed among some of the ancient nations 
of cutting off the hair and casting it on the body or into 
the tomb. So did the Roman women on Yirginta; so

The poorest man in Israel, when his wife died, never 
had less than two pipes, and one mourning woman,. 
Thus wwuming became an art^ which devolved on women 
A of shrill voices, copious of tears, and skilful in lamenting 
and praising the dead m mournful songs and eulogies. 
On a signal firom the chief mourner, these mourning wo- 
men took the chksf part, and the real mourners remained 
comparatively silent. So in ancient times. 

In modem days the nflSst sedate of all people, the 
Chinese, on the occasion of a funeral, burst out into loud 
shrieks and lamentations. All along the Levant also the 
practice of keening is in full vogue. Buckhardt, in his 
travels, tells us tluit a particular class of women is called 
in on the occasion of a death, whose sole profession is 
that of howling, in the most heart-rending accenU, for a 
small sum paid to them bj the house, Medina being the 
only town where this custom did not prevail. At Yembo, 
where the plague was raging, he heard when he retired 
to test, innumerable voices breaking out on all sides mto 
heart49reakmg and dreadful cries, which kept him awake 
the whole night. . 

This practice seems, uideed, universal in the East.

« The keen commences. The women :of the house- 
hold range themselves on either side of the bed, rise with

•• The rapidity and ease with which both the blessings 
and curses of the keen are uttered,and the epigramma- 
tic force of each concluding stana, generally bring tean 
mto the eyes of the most indifierent spocUtor, or produce

the scene is very powerful : the darkness of the death 
chamber, illumined only by candles that glare upon the 
corpse— the manner of repetition, or acknowledgment 
that runs round when the keener gives a sentence— the 
deep, yet suppressed sobs of the nearer roktives, and the

band, when allusion is made to the domestic virtues of the

•gain, and so on, each stana ending in the wail.

From the old classical epithet of ** black veiled king of 
the dead/' one would suppose that black had been uni- 
versally, as with ourseKeSf the mouming color. Not 
■Ot however. Plutarch writes that in their mourning, 
women kid aside tJieir purple, gold and Jewelty, and 
clothed themselves in white, " like as then the dead body 
was wrapped in white clothes. This color was thought 
the Bttestf becaose it Is clear, pure, and sincere, and least 
defiled." 

So in our own country (England) some white^ an em* * 
blem of purity, is alwnp displayed on the hearse and 
j^lt of a child or unmarried person. In the northern 
parts of England, indeed, a white linen scarf or hat>'band 
it an indispensable part of mourning for the dead at any 

Coarse red hempen cloth is the only dress allowed in 
China for the first and deepest mourning. In time tliis 
is changed to white ; and silk may be worn in half* 
mourning, but blue or white sleeves are indispensable. 
While being choaen as expressive of the belief that the 
dead are in heaven, the place of purity. So more prac- 
tical ly in Egypt yellow ts chosen, because it represents 
natural decay as exhibited in fruits and flowers ; whilst 
in Turkey blue is often adopted to denote the sky as the 
place of departed spirits. All this, however, whether in 
good or bod taste, is moveable mouming; but we are 
told that tlie first duty of the women of Medina, on as- 
mining mouming, is to d^e ike hands with indigo, . *

robed mates and weepen. It ia wrtmg that this pomp 
and circnmstance should be so engrafted on our national 
habits, that tlie desolate widow, the penniless orphan, or 
unportioned sister, muH cruelty embarrass themselves to 
obtain the precious vestments which custom dictates, or 
be supposed to fail in respect to the husband, the fiither, 
the brother, whom they loved in their heart of hearts, and 
to a re-union with whom they look as their chiefest hope 
and comfort 

The mourning which Christ hath hallowed — for h€ 
weft for Laxanu — has no communion with crape bands 
and weepers. There is no teacher^ like. Death. In his 
dread presence the great mysteiy of life opens on the 
sorrowing heart, the awakened mind. He teaches that 
fiuth and hope bj which the bruised seed is bound, the 
broken heart healed ; and as fragrance, wliich in its per- 
fectness was unknown, emanates from an herb when it is 
crushed, so does sorrow develope virtues and consolations 
undreamt of in gaj and happy hours. 

Thus does the (kithftil mpumer learn that sorrow and 
pain and suffering — those ** many waters," which threat* 
ened but did not overwhelm — passed, the purified and 
renewed spirit will emerge on that happier shore, where 
•in and sorrow are unknown, where tears are wiped from 
every eye, and where the toil-worn, grief-worn, stricken, 
but contrite denizen of the earth, shall stand blessed, pure 
and happy as a little child, in the presence of his Creator. 

And so chastened and subdued, and passing ** cheerly 
on through prayer unto the tomb," tlie true mourner 
looks beyond that solemn vestibule, to re-union with those 
deeply and enduringly loved on earth, who are— not 
lost ^ but gone before.

I 4i 



some wisdom in endeavoring to win a monument, if it 
were always tlie reward only of real merit and of virtu- 
ous actions. So pleasing is the idea of being honored by 
succeeding generations, Uiat one who is striving through 
persecution, neglect, poverty, and disgrace, to accomplish 
some great good for his fellow men, might feel compensa- 
ted by the reflection that after death his actions would be 
recorded with praise upon a conspicuous marble edifice. 
Bfit those who lie_ under sumptuous monuments aft er 
Jhg g death, arej jiff tfim" that dF**^* 'n mansions andpaF" 
aces wtule they were living. These marble piles have 
too olUSU Imvu i>KCl6d irom ihe'same motives that prompt 
the courtier to flatter those who are exalted by their po- 
sition ; and society has learned that the inscriptions upon 
them, like the flattery of the living, are often without 
truth. 

The simple headstone is seldom intended for anything 
more than an object by which the firiends might identify 
the spot where the remains of the dead repose. It is 
commonly a mere tribute of friendship, designed not to 
exaggerate the worth of the dead, but to pay a tender re- 
spect to his memoiy. It tells that the buried one had 
firiendi who wished to keep him in remembrance, and to 



ORAVSB WITHOUT 

bear an afiectionate testimony to his virtues. When, there- 
fore, we look upo n_ a simple headstone, we feel sensible 
of no attempt to exalt the dead by factitious honors : we 
feel t hat every good citiaen is w orthy of this humble 
fribute ; and the record o^ his name and age, o^ the time 
^ his death, and some few important circumstances of 
his history, may interest not only the living friends, but 
likewise die posterity of the departed, when the present 
generation has passed away. 

But when we see a grave without a stone, we know 
that the person buried there was either friendless, or 
that his friends were too indigent to pay this last respect 
to his obsequies. To a person of susceptible feelings and 
lively imagination, a mere suggestion is ofbn better than 
a full account of the circumstances of an event. When 
we examine a grave tliat is marked by a stone, the in- 
scription commonly satisfies our curiosity in relation to 
the dead. The name, the age, the Birth-place, and per- 
haps the character of the occupant are there recorded. 
'We turn away satisfied, and read the next epitaph. As 
we proceed we arrive at a green hillock that stands alone 
and is unmarked by a stone. The rising of the ground 
denotes the character of the spot. It makes known that 
the remains of a human being are there deposited, and 
the imagination is left to conjecture the cause of the 
neglect that has attended him in his sepulture. 

It may be the tomb of a stranger; of one who was 
lost from his fiunily and friends, who have never heard of 
his death, who are still seeking for him, and waiting his 
return. He may have been the only son of a widowed 
mother, who lives in a foreign land, and who still daily 
oflers prayers to heaven for his safety and his restoratkm. 
She wonders at his long absence, and dreams not that he 
lies here in a stranger's grave, which can never be known 



u hii retting place, and wliere no hnnuun being can 
Identify hit femaint. How many tears have been waited 
Ibat ahonld bave been poured as a sacred offering upon 
ibis green turf I How many coi\jectures bare alternately 
raised the bopes and depressed the spirits of this bereaved 
mother, wbich might have been tranquillixed, had she but 
Mowed the remains of this unlcnown slumberer to bis 
final xestl 

Many are the dead who lie in these nameless graves, 
and many who died with a pang of deep sorrow in their 
hearts, when they thought that they were fiur from those 
who knew them, and that their place of burial could 
never be recognised. Some perhaps would be dishonored 
by their own history: but bow many are they whose 
lives would awaken in our hearts the most tender emo- 
tbns, the liveliest interest in their affecting adventures, 
and die deepest sympathy in their misfortunes. The ob- 
■curity in which Uiese unknown tenants of the grave is in- 
Tolvedi affixes a romantic interest to their biography. No 
«nvy is excited by a vjgw of their lonely resting place, as 
when a sumptuous marble crowns the sepulchre of one 
whom we knew and who was undeserving of honor. It 
was with a ^w to this escape from the envy of the 
world, dial the youthihl poet wrote these lines :— 

«< Thus unlamented, let me die. 
Steal from the world, and not a stone, 
Tell where I Ue.** 

Bven those who during their life-time are desirous of 
fiume and position, feel that a monumental pile, be it ever 
io unpretending, is calculated to excite the envy of the 
world, and that after all their struggles for distinction, a 
hnmble life, and an unambitious grave, are perhaps the 
sincerely honored. 



.:' 



There is a moral in tlioso facts which is worthy of docp 
study and rollcction ; that teaclios a losson of humility to 
tlio ambitions, and accords witli tho saying of Josus, tliat 
** ho tliat humUotli liimsolf shall bo exalted." It provus 
tliat wo do not dovato tlio dead by piling up costly hon- 
ors on thoir graves, and that wo may indeed exalt tlio 
tnily groat by tlic simplicity with which we mark the 
placo of their sepulture. Whon one is laid in the ground, 
an unpretending stone with simply his name and age ro- 
cordcd upon it, and a few lines referring modestly to his 
cliarector and his virtues, witlioutany ostentatious brevity, 
end without direct or implied eulogy, attracts the atten- 
tion and wins the approbation of all. If he attained 
great distinction in life, some may think iliat ho deserved 
more honor after his death ; but if he wore truly great 
and worthy, his reputation is safer in tlio memory and 
affections of his countrymen, than upon tho glittering 
- marble that towers above his grave. ' -^^ 

The mo9t interesting graves, indeed, are those which 
are vrithout a stone. What is more picturesque than a ^ 
little hillock rising up among tho herbage, tliat marks 
the grave of an in&nt I And how easily might tliis 
charm be destroyed by a few of those accompaniments 
which vanity or bad taste might have caused the surviv- 
ing friends to heap up around it I At the sight of this 
diminutive grave we say, how often has this green turf 
been sprinkled by the tears of some indigent motlicr, 
wlio wept tho more because her tears were all tlie gift 
she had to bestow upon the grave of her beloved and 
lost. A monument may bo the cold offering of duty, 
when there were no affection and no sorrow ; but the tear- 
drops of a sincere mourner sanctify tlie spot, and make it 
blessed forever I

CATACOMBS. 



THE CATACOMBS OF ROU& 
Emtomiikd nox nu Aruumo Morhilt. 

Thb Roman Catacombs consist, for the most part, of a 
subterranean labyrinth of passages, cut tlirough tho soft 
Tolcanic rock of the Oampa|;na, so narrow as rarely to 
admit of two persons walking abreast easily, but here 
and there, on dther side, opening into chambers of vary- 
ing siae and form. The walls of tho passages, through 
their whole extent, are lined with narrow excavations, 
one above anotlier, large enough to admit of a body 
being placed in each ; and when they remain in tlieir 
original condition, these excavations are closed in front 
by tiles, or by a slab of marble cemented to the rock, 
and in most cases bearing an inscription. . Frequently 
there are several stories connected with each odier by 
sloping ways. 

There is no single circumstance, in relation to the cat- 
acombs, of more striking character than tlieir vast extent. 
About twenty different catacombs are now known, and 
are more or less open, — and a year is now hardly likely 
to pass witliout the discovery of a new one ; for the 
original number of underground cemeteries, as ascer* 
tained from the early authorities, was nearly, if not 
quite, tliree times this number. It is but a very few 
years suice the entrance to the famous catacomb of St. 
Callixtns, one of the most interesting of all, was found 
by the Cavaliere de Rossi ; and it was only in the Spring 
of 182^, that the buried church and catacomb of St. 
Alexander, on tlie Nomentan Way, were brought to light 
Earthquakes, floods, and neglect, have obliterated the 
openings of many of their ancient cemeteries — and the 



hollow soil of the Gampagna is full <*of hidden graves, 
which men walk over without knowuig where they are." 
Each of tlie twelve great highways which ran from 
tlie gates of Rome was bordered on eitlier side, at a short 
distance from tlie city wall, by tlio hidden Christian cemo- 
^ tcries. The only one of tlic catacombs, of wliich even 
a partial survey lias been made, is tliat of St. Agnes, of 
a portion of which tlie Padre Marclii published a map in 
1845. ** It is calculated to contaui about an eiglitli part 
of that cemetery. The greatest length of tho portion 
thus measured is not more than seven hundred feet, and 
its greatest widtli about five hundred and fifty ; never- 
theless, if we measure all the streets tliat it contains, 
tlieir united length scarcely falls short of two English 
miles. This would give fifteen or sixteen miles for all 
tlie streets in the cemetery of St. Agnes.*' Mr. Nortli- 
coto, from whose work the preceding paragraph is taken, 
estimates tlie total length of tlie catacombs at nine hun- 
dred miles. 

Taking the above account as a fair average of the size 
of the catacombs, for some are larger and some smaller, 
we most assign to tlie streets of graves already known a 
total length of about three hundrod miles, vriUi a proba- 
bility tliat the unknown ones are at least of equal length. 
. This conclusion appears startling when one thinks of 
tho close arrangement of the lines of graves along tho 
walls of these passages. The height of tlie passages 
varies greatly, and witli it tlie number of graves one 
above anotlier ; but tho Padro Marchi, who is competent 
authority, estimates the average number at ten ; that is, 
five on each side, for every seven feet, — which would 
give a popubtion of tho dead, for tlie tliree hundred miles, 
of not less than two millions and a quarter. No one who 
Iws visited tho catacombs can believe, surprising m this 



224 



MOUNT AUBUEBi 



number may seem, that tlie Padro Mardii*8 calculation 
is an extravagant one as to the number of grayes in a 
given space. The writer of this has counted eleven 
graves, one over another, on each side of the passage, 
and there is no space lost between the head of one grave 
and the foot of another. Everywhere tliere is economy 
of space, — the economy of men working on a hard ma- 
terial, difficult to be removed, and laboring in a confined 
space, with the need of haste. 

The question of the number of the dead in the cata- 
combs opens the way to many other curious questions. 
The length of time tliat the catacombs were used as 
burial pUces ; the probability of others, beside Christians 
being buried in tJiom ; tlio number of Christians at Rome 
during tlie first two centuries, in comparison with tho 
total number of the inliabitants of tlie city ; and how far 
tlio public profession of Christianity was attended wiUi

HEART BURIAL. 
Fbom ** CDBomom or tmm Tomm.** 

The custom of buiying the heart separately from tlie 
rest of the body prevailed in the sixteenth century, in 
the case of death at a distance from home. The body 
was deposited in a grave in the place where the person 
died, and the heart was sent homo to the friends of 
the deceased. Thusi in 1569, Sir Robert Pecicham, 
dying at Rome, his body was buried at St. Gregory in 
that city, and his heart at Durham Church in England. 

Mr. Steele, an early writer, relates the following inci- 
dent in connection with the burial. ** As I came accident- 
ally into the church, Sept 25, 1711, a vault at tlie east 
end of the north isle being opened, into which I went, 
and found a small box of lead Cushioned like a heart, but 
flat, being scarce two niches in thickness, with the lead 
sawdered, but the under part corroded ; tlie heart of Sir 
Robert Peckham discovered itself, wrapped within several 
cloths, and still smelling strong of the embalmment." On 
the lid was his inscription.

HKFUBUCAN BURIAL. 



238 



The true principle of republican burial is to be con- 
tent that all the dead should remain as obscure as tlicy 
were in their lifetime. It is absurd to endeavor to exalt 
a person, who has lived all his life in obscurity, whoso 
very name was only known to his neighbors and to tlioso 
with whom he had coinmereial dealings, by the art of tho 
sculptor. It is nothing to tlie purpose tliat he was in 
truth a greater and more virtuous man than the occu|)ant 
of the next grave, who is honored by a tower of gi-anito 
or a marble effigy, on account of his distinguished position 
as a hero or a statesman. The lost belongs to liistor}', 
and however mean his virtues or his talents, the nation 
will always bo interested in liis biography. It is no anti- 
republican principle, therefore, which admits tlie propriety 
of erecting a costly monument to a man of ordinaty worth 
and talents, who has been a President of tho United 
States, and which docs not admit the ex]K>diency of build- 
ing such a tribute to an obscure citizen of the highest 
viituc, who performed no public acts. Let tho stone that 
ninrks his grave give humble testimony of his virtues, 
and in proportion to iu humility will the reader who 
stoops to read his epitaph, believe it to be true. 

'i*o posterity a monument containing the name of ono 
whose nnnio or deeds cannot bo traced in tho literary, 
civil, or ecclesiastical history of his time, must be regard- 
ed in the saino light as one containing a fictitiom 
name. There could exist no motive to preserve such a 
monument, unless on account of tlio oxtremo scarcity of 
such works, it might be valued as a curiosity and a relic 
of antiquity. In the eighteenth century, in tliis country, -^ 
almost every private name was connected with the public 
events of tho time. Wo have, tlicrcforo, a strong motive 
to preserve every monument of their dead, both on ac- 
count of their historical iuqrartance, and their value as 



MOUNT AUBURN. 

antiquities. Sach circumstances no longer exist in this 
country, and can never exist heroafVer. Henceforth, 
those few only, who are the most distinguished among 
the prominent men of the age, can afford any interest to 
posterity. The discoverer of a new world, or of a new 
science, tlie leader of some great moral or political revo- 
lution, — the Father Mathew, or the Wilbcrforce of his 
own time, — such names alone amidst the tens of thou- 
sands of men who are great by position, who are great in 
an ordinary way, will bo noticed or even remembered by 
a succeeding generation. The remainder, will bo like so 
many names upon a vast and interminable catalogue. 

Who is this man, posterity will inquire, with a tower 
of stone erected over his remains, whose works and deeds 
ara not recorded even in the 'local history of his own 
neighborhood ? Whosoever he might be, his monument 
does but record and publish his obscurity and inferiority 
to a succeeding generation. The philosophy of that sort 
of reputation which is gained after one*sdeAtli by marble 
tower or effigy, if properly studied, would demonstrate 
that one might as well attempt to scale the heavens by 
another tower of Babel, as to purchase it of the sculptor. 

A wealthy citixen in the town of , had lately 

buried his ikther, who was a man of rare excellence of 
character. He erected a plain headstone over his grave, 
and recorded in a few touching lines a testimony of his 
worth and of his own veneration for his memory. His 
neighbors inquired why he had paid so little respect to 
the memory of his father, whom all the citizens of the 
place would delight to honor, by the proudest work of 
sculpture. ** My father,*' replied the son, '* lived all his 
days in obscurity, doing good witliout ostentation, seeking 
no honors, but satisfied with the approbation of his own 
eonidence, and with the pleasure it afforded him to tliink 



'.■^ 



TUB PAUPEK*8 DBATIt-BED. 

of the happiness he had conferred upon others. Were I to 
erect a costly monument over his remains, I should per- 
form an act whicli would be inconsistent with the tenor 
of his life, and tlie principles of his conduct He lived 
a humble life, and he is honored by tliis humble grave, 
and by the filial testimony which is recorded of his virtues. 
His deeds are embalmed in tlie memory of hundreds 
whom he has reformed, blessed, and alleviated ; and this 
plain headstone honors his memory more than the proud- 
est column of marble.*'

TREES IN MOUNT AUBURN. 

It is not generally uTidcrstood that there may bo too 
many trees, as well as too many flowers, in a rural ccmo- 
tcry, too many for tlie beauty as well as for tlie conveni- 
ence of tho place. When trees are crowded closely 
togetlier, tliey lose their lateral branches and all tlieir 
clianicteristic beauty. Ono broad-s))rcading treo tliat 
covers a largo s^mce of ground is more serviccablo for 
shade, and more beautiful and ottractivo in its ap^icarance, 
than ten or twelve tall, slender trees occupying tho same 
space. This njmark is particularly applicable to trees in 
cemeteries, in which it is desirable to obtain as great a 
canopy''of shade and foliage, with as little encumbrance 
from the roots and stems of trees, as can be made to 
subsist togetlier. The trunk of one broad-headed tree 
occupying tlie space of one or two feet in diameter, 
leaves the remainder of tho ground that is shaded by it 
free to bo used for a burial spot. A number of smaller 
trees occupying tlio same space fill it up so closely witli 
tlieir roots and stems, as to render it useless for the burial 
of tlie dead ; and though it will not bo denied that there 
is grandeur in a dense forest of such trees, there is vastly 
more of tliis quality in a grove of trees which are broad 
and perfect in tlieir shape. Tho first may bo compared 
to a hall witli a flat roof sustained by a largo number of 
small pillars ; the last to a roof consisting of a few noble 
arches resting on massive coluimis, leaving unoccupkxl a 
wide intermediate 8|Nice. 

Mount Auburn would bo at present a more lieautiful 
plocc, and more convenient for tho purposf^ to which it 
is dedicated, if, at tlic time of its consecration as a cemo- 



tcry, it had been entirely free from wood, and afterwards 
liad been judiciously planted with young trees of the 
prevailing species. Very few well formed trees are to bo 
seen in these grounds, becanso they are mostly the elon- 
gated trees of tho foixjst, which occupy a great deal of 
Rjiace in pro]K>rtion to tlie amount of sliade affonled by 
tlicin, and greatly encumber tlie bnrial lots. 

It may bo further remarked, that it is injnrious to tlie 
monuments to stand under tlie drip of trees, whkJi ouglit 
not, therefore, to grow inside of tlie burial lots ; the only 
trees that ought to be planted near tlie lots are such as do 
not widely extend eitlier their roots or their branches. 
Such aro the different species of the arhw t7to,and other 
coniferous trees that acquire a slender pyramidal shape. 
The advantages of trees in a cemetery cannot be enjoyed 
witliout a few attendant evils ; but the latter might in some 
measure be avoided, if the lai^r kinds of trees were con- 
fined to tlie avenues and to certain* tracts whkrh art) not 
to bo usod for the burial of the dead. 

The avenues, to answer this end, should be made of 
sufficient width to permit a row of large trees to stand 
and spread their branches freely on each side. The foot- 
paths, on account of their narrow width, should bo 
hordcrcd only with shrubbery and trees of a slender, 
spiry growtli. Tlie elm and the oak, which require great 
amplitude of space, ought to be extirpated from all nar- 
row and confined situations. 

Tlie idea of attaining picturesque effects in a rural 
cemetery, by tlie grouping of trees, cannot be carried 
into practice. The necessary formality that must pre- 
vail in tlio construction of the patlis and avenues, and 
in tho geometrical forms of tho burial lots, especially 
when tliey are enclosed by a fence, prevento any such 
groupings and combinations. A formal irregularity is 



840 



UOVMT AUBUSM. 



no more picturesque than any otbcr kind of formality. 
The wild and rather pleasing disorder apparent in Uie 
natural arrangement of the trees in Mount Auburn, is 
every year becoming obliterated, as the proprietors cut 
down the trees in the lote and leave those only in the 
paths and avenues. As often as a new proprietor lays 
out a burial lot, he is obliged to destroy all or nearly all 
the trees within its bounds. Thoy must at last, therefore, 
be confined almost entirely to the avenues, forming rows 
that correspond to tlieir directions, and exhibiting in 
their disposition the same irregukr formality. But as 
the remaining trees will increase in brcadtli, in propor- 
tion as their number is lessened, tlio grounds will con- 
tinue to be as well shaded as tlicy are at tlie present 
time, and will bo improved in grandeur and beauty. 

It is apparent that in many cases, eidier some fine 
trees must bo sacrificed, or tlio burial lot must be devoted 
to the trees instead of the graves. A great deal of judg- 
ment must bo required to determine when it would be 
expedient to reserve tlie lot in order to save a tree. If 
the UUtcr be young, vigorous, and of good projwrtions, it 
ought to be traiisplantc?d- into a convenient and appro- 
priate locaUon ; if it bo too lai^go to be removed, the 
value of the tioo should decide ito fate. The fate which 
must, at some not very distant period, come upon the 
trees now witliin the lots, might suggest the expediency 
of pUnting trees near them in tlie avenues, in anticiim- 
tion of it. The young trees thus planted would supply 
the places of the old growtli as it is removed ; and ex- 
hibit superior sixe and beauty. Twenty years hence, the 
aspect of Mount Auburn will be less wild ; it will have 
less of tlio peculiar attractions of a forest ; but if nothing

FUNEREAL CHARACTERS OP TREES. 

Mount Auburn was originally selected for a ceme- 
tery, on account of the beauty and variety of its primitive 
forest growth, no less than for its pleasing diversity of 
surface. The greater part of the indigenous species of 
Massachusetts may be found here. The native shrubs 
were also numerous in the original grounds ; but these 
have been nearly extirpated, to make room for foreign 
shrubs. The proprietors of lots have generally prefoned 
Uio kttcr ; according to tlie principle that governs them 
in trade, — namely, that the most valuable article is the 
one that bears tlie highest price in the market. Hence 
tlie dwarf kalmia, one of the most beautiful of nature's 
productions, and the different cornels and viburnums, 
must resign their places to altheas, smoke plants, and 
Judas trees. 

In selecting locations for otlier rural cemeteries, a 
similar regard lias boon paid to trees, which aro con- 
sidurtxl mdispcnsablo at the outset; but how well soever 
tlio place may be diversified with trees, many will necea- 
sarily be removed for convenience, and otiiers will need 
to bo planted to fill vaconckis, and to supply the want 
of certain valuable species. A cemetery witliout trees 
would be very blank and unaUractive, however well 
supplied with flowers. Jiut all kinds are not equally 
well adapted to this situation; somo being remarkable 
for certain funereal diaracters and associations, while 
others are fitted for a cemetery from their advantageous 
manner of growth. Deciduous trees are to be preferred

NEATNESS OF THE GEOUNDS. 

— ru*-, 

One of tlie most important points in the management 
of a rural cemetery, is to preserve throughout the year a 
neatness of the grounds : not that every path and every 
lot should look as if the gardener witli his spade and 
shears was constantly at work ; but ratlior that tlie patlis 
should contain no unsightly weeds, and that tlio lots 
sliould bo free from all litter and rubbisli. At present 
tliis neatness is preserved only by means of a great deal 
of expensive labor; and this labor being frequently 
omitted in certain cases, tlie weeds and decayed stalks of 
plants accumulate and become offensive to the sight 
When wo go into the wild pastures wo see but little of 
tills tangled and unsightly appearance, which seems, 
tliercforo, to bo the result of tillage. In the pastures tlie 
plants that appear first in tlie season are not so luxuriant 
as the later ones, and the decayed stalks of tho early 
|iiants are therefore covered and concealed by tlie more 
exuberant growth of tlioso which succeed them ; and 
not until the hard autumnal frosts arrive, do we see any 
accumulations of decayed plants. It is not so in grounds 
which nre subjected to tillage, causing early in tlio sea- 
son a luxuriant crop of pknts, and forming a mass of 
decayed stalks and fuliagu at an early period. Tlio cnl« 
tivated grasses grow with so niiich luxui'iunco that they 
must bo frequently cut in order to preserve a neat surface 
in a garden or a cemetery. 

The remedy for this evil and tliis expense is to imrsue 
a system of embellishment tliat requires no tillage. At 
present the greatest eflTort is' made to preserve a smooth 
shaven lawn, and to cultivate the best lawn grasses. 



] 



25d 



MUL'NT AlULRN. 



These require, from the first, a deep and fertile soil : 
otherwise tlie native and more vigorous grasses would 
quickly supersede them, or ^ run them out*' To keep 
them in good condition, — to preserve anytliing like an 
appearance of neatness, — tliey must be cut six or eight 
times during the season ; and in a place h'ke Mount Au- 
hum, the scythe cannot bo used, and the gardener must 
perform almost Uie whole labor with the shears or the 
pruning hook. If the gmsses are allowed to remain un- 
cut unlil they have flowered, they cover the ground with 
their stiff stalks, and spoil the verdure and softness of the 
surface. The aggregate expense of keeping all the grass 
of the cemetery in proper condition, from May to Sejv 
tember, is immense , and the larger tlie proportion of 
lawn grasseb and other cultivated plants, the greater the 
amount and expense of the labor required to keep the 
place in order. The consequence of this difficulty and 
expense is, that certain portions of tlie grounds are fre- 
quently neglected, and too often present a tangled growth 
of hevhB and grasses from midsummer till the end of the 
year. 

All this trouble might bo avoided, if tlie enclosures 
were covered with sods taken from tlie wild upland pas- 
tures, in which there is a large proportion of lichens and 
r'^*^** I mosses, that fumisli a bed for the wild flowers, and check 
the luxuriance of the grasses. When these turfs are 
used, they should be laid upon a thin and natural soil, 
and all the rank species of wild grass should be eradi- 
cated. If they are placed upon a good garden soil, that 
has been well composted, tlio herbs of luxuriant growth 
will ««kill oat" all the mosses and wild flowers. The 
ktter require a certain amount of shade and protection, 
but not a deep nor fertile soil. If, instead of an iron 
fence, the lota were tnrrounded with a miscellaneous

growth of wild shrubbery, the ground would receive 
that sort of shade and protection which nature afibrds 
the flowers and mosses in the wild lands, and a constant 
verdure would be produced by the mosses, and a contin- 
ued succession of flowers. The grounds m the lots 
would not require any culture : nothing more would be 
necessary than to eradicate by the hand sudi plants as 
are too luxuriant, and those of a tliomy description. No 
spade, nor scythe, nor sliears, would need to be used from

pense of tillage. 

The principal labor which would be required is to 
procure and lay down the sods, and in some cases^ when 
the soil was not favorable, to renew Uie sods, which 
ought, in all coses, to be taken froip upland tracts, because 
it is presumed tliat the burial lots, if originally wet, are 
made artificially dry. The cemetery, if universally 
treated in this manner, would wear tlie appearance of 
iwturo witliout its redundances — an appearance which 
would be delightful to all, and confessedly superior to 
the present highly dressed and artiflcial look, combined 
with a weedy and tangled growth in those places which 
have been neglected a few days. Some people associate 
a tangled and weCdy appearance with wild lands : but a 
close observation would prove to tlicm their mistake. 
This appearance is seen in a neglected garden and other 
tilled lands, but seldom or never in lands winch are in a 
state of nature. 

I would admit into a rural cemetery, no work at all for 
the scythe or tlie shears, if it be possible to avoid it. 
The spade should also finish its work, after having com- 
pleted the grave, covered it and sodded the surfiMe. I

MONUMENTAL TREES. 

•• This to tlM boirar sb* 1ot«d,

In lome parts of tlie continent of Europe, parents, in 
in compliance with an ancient custom, are in the habit 
of planting a tree at the birtli of every child. This tree 
is ever afterwards identified with the individual for 
whom it was planted ; it is associated with his life, and, 
when he is dead, it is viewed by his friends and com- 
panions as a living monument to his memory. I have 
often thought that we might derive from this custom a 
hint, to be turned to an important advantage ; that, in 
in the place of marble, our departed friends nught Jbe 
commenwj^ that should, every year ■ 

when it put forth its leaves, awsflcen fresh memories of 
the dead. After tlio remains of a friend are laid in the 
grave, a tree should be selected, not ui the cemetery, 
but in our own grounds, and dedicated to his memory. 
A cenotaph placed in the ground, near the tree, should 
indicate the dedication of the troo to the memoiy of 
the person whoso name is recorded upon the stone. On 
that spot it should ever afterwards be allowed to remain, 
and no profane hands should venture to disturb it. 

How much more noble a monument would such a tree~7 
afford than the sculptured marble. There must be some 
satis&ction in the thought, that, after we are laid in the 
grave, we are still doing good to our fdlow-men. A 
monumental stone, while it commemorates the dead, 
encumbers the ground on which it stands. A tree, on- 
the contrary, is constantly performing a useful office, in. 
the economy of nature, (or all living creatures. To the' 



204 



MOUNT AUBXmK. 



Cftrth, and to the crettores that find shelter under it, it 
is a guardian angel of nature. A century hence, if 
the land should bo decorated with millions of these 
monumental trees, shading the earth, and holding up 
their arms to heaven, whence they call down a perpetual 
store of blessings to the living, how would posterity 
revere tho custom that had saved so many from destruc- 
tionl 

Trees, when thus consecrated, might bo regarded as 
the medium of constant messages from tlie dead to the 
living, who might view in one of these trees tlie emblem 
of some of the transcendent joys of heaven. See how it 
is constantly shedding blessings and bounties upon the 
earth, many of which are unpereeived, like tlioso we 
receive from the .hand of Providence. It puts forth its 
leaves in the spring, affording a beautiful image to look 
upon, and purifying and renovating the vital atmosphere, 
and in the autumn it sheds it leaves to serve as a warm 
covering for the flowers in winter. The birds that sing 
in its branches do but communicate' those pleasing 
thoughts that cannot "tw expressed in words, but serve 
to awaken in our hearts a gleam of those joys which 
are felt by the blessed in heaven. When we sit under 
its shade in summer, we feel as if overshadowed by an 
angel's wings, so musically do the sephyrs, as they play 
through the leaves and branches, whisper of tlie world of 
the past and the heaven of the futiure. It is pleasing to 
think, that when our friends are sitting after our death 
under this canopy of shade, we may be remembered by 
them, and that the tree tliat commemorates our life is 
the source of constant benefits to our fellow-men, who 
have not yet passed through tlie gate of mortality. 

Were the custom of adopting trees as monuments to 
our departed firiends to be adopted, the 



MOKUXKMTAL 



265 



most enduring species should bo selected for this purpose, 
such as the elm, the oak, the ash, the lime, and the 
maple. The soft-wooded trees are not in general suffi- 
ciently durable, and might decay before the friends Of 
the dead had passed away. After the selection of a tree 
for this purpose, a small slab should bo set down into the 
earth, near tlie trunk of the tree, and, on the face of it, 
these words, or otliers of similar import, should be in- 
scribed : — " To the memory of C. L., whose remains 
are laid in Mount Auburn, diis lime-tree, under whoso 
shade she lias often reclined, is afToctionatcly dedicated. 
May no profane hands ever destroy it, or disturb the 
birds that sing among' its branches. Henceforth this 
tree, with its winged inhabitants, is sacred to her 
memory.*' 

The general prevalence of a custom like this would 
lead the public to place a higher value upon trees, and 
less upon showy monuments of marble, which have 
always been tho most sumptuous in the caily stages of 
civilization, ^s m ankind advance in civilizatjon_or. wr, 
intellect, they prize nature more and art less. In a veiy 
exalted stage of gcncaraTsocial culture, sumptuous monu- 
ments would become extremely rare. 

We should not neglect to consider tliat every tree thus 
hallowed and preserved becomes a public benefiiction. 
The rude purveyor of the lumber market, who measures — ^^ " 
the value of a tree only by the surveyor's guage, and 
who is never at peace as long as a single old oak or 
elm, or other valuable timber tree is standing, which can 
bo purchased with money, would know tliat the curse of 
the whole community would bo upon him, if he allowed 
his venal hands to touch one of these monumental trees. 
Religion and affection might succeed in preserving what 
reason, and taste, and philanthropy, have advocated in

vain for tlie last centaiy. Men who have no regard for 
a tree as one of the noblest works of nature, and one of 
the greatest gifts of heaven to mankind, who have not 
science enough to understand its value in the econom j 
of nature, nor taste enough to appreciate its beauty, may 
ieel some religious respect for an object that has been 
selected to commemorate the death of a friend. Posteri^, 
who may take an invidious satisfaction in destroying the

heir4ooms from their ancestors. And how vastly more 
noUe a monument to any one*s memory, a century hence, 
must an elm be regarded, which is the pride of every

tored effigy in Westminster Abbey.

TiHB hatli a mighty power, to which we all must bend. 
He tears away tlie young, tlie loved, Uie beautiful, from

heart. Yet, strange to say, we look upon tlie wrecks 
which ho Ims made, with an intensity of interest, which 
the days of their glory could never have awakened. We 
reverence the hooiy head, for revolutions have passed 
over it, mysterious and strange. We gaze with deep 
and unchanging emotions upon tlie silver hair whidi his 
touch has whitened, for it fidls over a brow that possesses 
a greater charm, because it is hidden from our knowledge. 

We look upon the magnificent structures of the artist 
with delight, because they awaken in us that nice per- 
ception of the beautiful, which affords us exquisite pleas- 
ure. We admire the beauty of the design, and the taste 
and skill displayed in tlie execution ; but never does the 
structure in its meridian glory become as dear to us, as 
when the frosts of time have beautified and adorned it ; 
when it becomes a part of the past, and linked witli deeds 
of high device, and manly daring, witli gentle tales of the 
afiecUons, of bower and hall, of heart and lute. We 
admire it at first, we love it at last It comes uito tht*

Mississippi is full of proofs tliat it was once inhabited by 
a race of men, civilised and enlightened like ourselves. 
There are remains of ancient cities and fortifications; 
there are implements of war and of husbandry; tliere 
arc proofs of matliematical skill and of science, which 
could never have been possessed by the rude and uncul- 
tured tribes whwh the encroachments of the white men 
have displayed. Yet so far back in the vista of time do

tlie race that left them for us. Did tliey exist in one 
fiortion of this great valley alone, there might be less to

numerous as the territory itself, and furnish the most 
conclusivo proofs, that it was once fiir more thickly pop- 
ulated than now. Bricks, medals, and vessels of various 
kinds, have been discovered in a soil which has been un- 
disturbed for ages. Hieroglyphic characters have been 
found inscribed in various places ; implcmento of war, of 
singular and difficult construction ; wells stoned up; re- 
gular walls; everything wliich could be necessaiy to 
prove the existence of a great and enlightened people.

tiyinto three classes, and attributes these remains of

INTERIOR BEAUTIES OF MOUKT AUBURN. 

Iv treaUng of the interior beauties of Mount Auburn, 
I shall confine my remarks cliiofly to the natural scenery. 
Every visitor must notice that the grounds are remark- 
able for a great and beautiful diversity of surface, as well 
as a large variety of trees. Long before any ono tliought 
of it as a cemetery, it was a favorite resort of students 
and others who sought it on account of its beautiful and 
romantic scenery. There are hundreds still living, who 
remember it as tlie scene of many a delightful ramble, 
alone and with company. It was no vidgar place of re- 
creation ; but a spot whither the thoughtful and studious 
fled for that needful rest from menUl weariness, or for 
employing themselves in those studies of nature which 
are even better than entire repose from labor. 

Many a student has resorted to these grounds for his 
first botanical explorations, as they were the nearest m 
(^^''ihe vicinity of Uie Colleges, where there was any remark- 
able variety of native plants. In these protected dales, 
the spring flowers made their earliest appearance ; and 
m the same places, under the shelter of tlie old oak-trees, 
lingered Uie latest flowers of autumn. The name which 
was given to the place, is associated with some of the 
most interesting -poetic images, and served to endear it 
still more to the readers and admirors of the most de- 
lightful poem in the language. Every spot within the 
grounds became at length hallowed in their remembrance. 
Every stream had its goddess, and every fountain its 
Naiad. Every wooded hill seemed to be the haunt of 
the Muse, and some rustic deity presided in every grove. 
Then aro many persons who may regret the changes 
which have taken place. When any spot is thus en- 



IIITBBIOB BBAYJTIBS OF MOUNT AUBUBK. 



277 



deared by memory, we delight in preserving its original 
appearance. Everything about it is sacred ; and evoiy 
alteration that might be highly gratifying to a stranger, 
is painful and displeasing to those who were fiimiliar witli 
it in their early days. 

When we examine tlie natural bcauUes of Mount Au- 
burn, we can easily account for all these attachments, 
and do not wonder that in tlio eyes of hundreds, it was 
regarded as hallowed ground, long before it was conse- 
crated to the dead. Some of those who delighted to 
ramble here aro now dead, and their dust is deposited in 
tlicso grounds and amidst the scenes which tliey loved 
in their lifetime. Among the living Uicro are numk-rs 
who regard the spot wiUi still more affection, since it lias 
been thus consecrated, and who, when they visit t?ie 
place, behold tlie scene of many a pleasant and studious 
excursion, while tliey view the graves of tlie companions 
with whom they were associated in their adventures. 
Tlie grounds are, therefore, hallowed in a twofold sense, 
and their original beauties have received a double charm ; 
first from the pleasing recollections of youth which they 
awaken, and second, firom han'ng become tlie depository 
of the sacred relics of early friends and comrades. 

It is at tlie beginning of summer, and the middle of 
autumn, that the lover of forest scenery would find the 
most pleasure in a general view of the natural beauties of 
Mount Auburn. The trees, as I have remarked in 
anotlier place, have but little individual beauty ; but in 
their collective beauty, when viewed from a near emi- 
nence, they are unsurpawcd. AVhen the leaves aro open- 
ing, before they liave assumed their deepest verdure, and 
when they are tinged with a paler shade of the tints that 
mark them just before tlie fidi of the leaf, Uie woods of 
the cemetery present to the eye of the spectator a very 



interesting variety. The oaks are particiilarly conspico- 
oos at this tune, as their sprouting leaves are seldom 
green, hut rather of a cinereous hue, intermingled with 
shades of red, purple, and lilac. The young leaves of the 
ash are generally of a deep purple, hecoming green as 
they advance towards maturity. The same may he said 
of ^e foliage of other trees which assume any of the 
shades of red and purple in tlie autumn. But all that 
remain green or turn yellow in the autumn, may he dis- 
tinguished in early summer hy the purity of their green 
tints, differing from their summer tint only hy its lighter 
shade. Here, then, are to he seen at this time, not 
only a charming variety in tlie different shades of ver- 
dure, hut another variety proceeding from the mixture of 
other colors. 

In the later summer tliese colors and shades have 
become blended into one nearly uniform dark shade of 
green, which attracts but little attention. About the 
third week in September, the ash, the maple, the tupelo 
and the sumach, begin to assume their bright autumnal 
metamorphosis — the ash varying from a salmon color to 
a deep chocolate or maroon, and the otiiers exhibiting all 
tlie shades between an orange and a scarlet. At this 
period very few lanrlscapes exceed the glorious display 
of colors which may be seen from the tower. 

There is not only a pleasing diversity of surfiice in 
Mount Auburn, but there is a remarkable correspond- 
ence in the laying out of the paths and avenues, with 
this diversity. There is no affected irregularity. The 
paths seem to take their course in the line of the inequal- 
ities of the ground, and the visitor can always find a 
suflBcient cause for every turn and bend of the principal 
avenues. In riding or walking over them wo meet with 
constant changes of scene. If there be a similarity in the
