279 



general style of tlie monuments, tliis sameness is com- 
pensated by the varied scenery. The ponds of Mount 
Auburn, though small, are a pleasing feature of the place. 
One of the most interesting portions of the ground, is a 
natural ridge, that passes over considerable space, from 
north to south. This has been named Indian Ridge. Tlie 
principal elevation is called Mount Auburn, and is one

River, and from the tower which is erected on its sum-

Water, in its ever varying and shifting forms, is an 
element of great beauty; and whether seen in the wide 
ocean, the rapid river, the quiet lake, or falling fountain, 
its presence wonderfully enhances the beauty of the sur- 
rounding scenery. Mount Auburn, until recently, has 
been nearly destitute of the fine imagery and iK)etio 
pictures which tlie presence of a large body of water 
produces ; but Meadow Pond, which has now been 
curbed and graded, supplies this deficiency, and has 
already become the most attractive resort in the cemetery, 
and the avenue which encloses its shores will soon be re- 
garded as the finest promenade within the grounds.

who owns the seat which I perceive at a distance. The

house may' also disfigure tlie reputation of the master. 
So congenial to the soul is the evidence of certain virtues, 
that we are delighted to see them emblemiaod in the 
works of nature and of art, and if this evidonco be want- 
ing in the artificial objects of a landscape, we fed no 
desire for the friendship of the pcoplo who are associated 
with them. ' ^^ ^ 

Of all sinister qualities (prid^ is the most easily mani- 
fested and the most despicable, when exhibited in works 
of art ; for men hate, even while they profess to admire, 
everything that arbitrarily exalts others above thcmsdvei. 
We dislike, in the dross, manners and conversation of a 
man, any appearance that plainly intimates his con- 
sciousness of superiority. Tliis rcinark is no disagreeable 
reflection upon human nature ; for it is not actual sup^ 
riority that we dislike, but the ostentation or counterfeit 
of it. We are led instinctively to feel assured that the 
afiectatiun of any quality is an evidence of the want of it. 
This is notoriously true of tlie affectation of wealth. 
Envy, which, after all, is but a hatred of fidse distinctioiis, 
not of real merit, —

bo entirely wanting. In orcliitecture and ictilptiiT« all 
depends on the mtinncr in which appendages intended 
for ornament *to displayed ; and whether the aasem- 
blege of parts seems to have been dictated by a Jove of 
beauty and propriety, or by emulation and a feeling of 
rivalry. In the marble of our ccDieteries, this rivalry is 
made apparent by the constantly increasing endeavers, 
on the part of the builders of new monuments, to omt^ 
do the most sumptuous and costly whicli are already 
completed. 

It 13 a maxim in the arts to arotd raising agreeable 
expectations which cannot be gratified. For this reason 
a perfect orator would avoid high-flown language, and a 
|>ompous address. He avoids raising expectations, in 
order that every charming lentlmentt every rational 
argument, and every happy turn of wit, may strike the 
hearer with an agreeable surprise, and pcnctmte more 
deeply into the mind* Pompous orators and ostentatious 
artists enjoy more notoriety : they are better ** stars ** { 
but their worfis and works produce no indelible impres- 
sion on the mind of the public ; their reputation is ephe- 
mcral i aud their works daxzle wiUiotit enlightvning tha 
community. 

The Qhallowness of such pretensions m more readily 
discovered in daily conversation, when tlio speaker or 
actor is frequently before us. All persons are pleased 
wiiU a plainly drc^ed man or woman, whoso maonen 
and conversation indicate a high degree of benevolence, 
intellect, and rednement. We note with plcasum the 
entire absence of any apparent intention to impose upon 
us by etiquette or by elegance of apparel. On the con- 
trary, when we are led by the elegant and costly dress 
of a woman, to expect a corresponding superiority of 
manaers, reAnoment, and edui^tion, and perhaps of per-

tonal beauty, and find, on introduction to her, a eonnte- 
nance of Tulgar expressions, and manners and conyersap 
tion that afiord incontestable proof of ignorance and low- 
breeding, we are afibcted with contempt There is manjr 
a qoalitj that becomes despicable only by position. Bad 
grammar and bad pronunciation may be associated with 
aome of the most noble virtues of the human character. 
In a laborer's cottage, they might not diminish our re- 
spect for the inmates ; but they become Contemptible 
when playing a part in a splendid mansion and in fiuh* 
ionablo costume. 

This principle is at the foundation of our dislike of a 
f structure that exhibits promises which, on close inspec- 
, it cannot fulfil ; as in a dwelling-house that appears 

general inspection to be built for hospitality, and on 
closer inspection betrays only meanness and pretence; 
or in a monument that seems to be- raised for the com- 
memoration of some distinguished benefactor of mankind, 
»-but is found on a near view, to be erected in celebra- 
tion of the fortunes oLjl living person who has risen to 
wealth, without any talents or virtues to distinguish him 
from others. Our love of truth afibcts our opinion of 
the arts as well as of human conduct. 

When expectations, but feebly excited, are suddenly 
rewarded by gratification, our pleasure is greatly magni- 
fied by our surprise. This happens, after contemplating 
the neatness and simplicity of a humble headstone, or 
tablet, when we read in the inscription, the name of 
iomo deceased person whose memory all delight to honor. 
The dead who. slumbers beneath it is not demeaned by 
this simple tribute to his memory ; and the spot becomes 
sanctified by those poetical associations that always hover 
round a tomb so humble and so picturesque. The name 
even of Washington may be demeaned by the ambitioa

ON THB AFFllCTIOKS'OF LIFE. 

Fkom ZiuvamAtWM 

Who has not in the moment of conrttl(i«cenee» in the 
hour o^ ntelancholy, or when sepamtion or death has de- 
prive*! one of the intercourses of friendahipt sought relief 
in the salutary shades of the country 1 Happy Is the 
being who is sensible of the advantages of a religions 
retirement from the world, of a sacred tranquillity, in 
which all the benefits to be derived from society impress 
tliem$elves more deeply in the heart, and every hour Is 
consecrated to the practice of the mild and peaceful vir- 
ttiesl But these advantages become much more 'con- 
spicuous, when we compare the modcji of thought which 
employ tlie mind of a solitary philosopher with those of 
a wor ldly sensual ist ; the tiresome and tumultuous life of 
the one with the soft tranquillity of the other £ when we 



i; 



896 



Momrr avbubn. 



oppose the fear and horror that distarb the death-bed of 
the warldljHninded man, irith the peaceable and easy 
exit of those pious souls who submit with resignation to 
the will of hearen. It is at this awful moment that we 
ftd the importance of turning the eye inwardly upon 
ourselves, if we would bear the sufferings of life with 
dignity, and the pains of death with resignation. 

Retirement aflfords us the most incontestable advan- 
tages, under the greatest adversiUes of life. The con- 
▼alescent, tlie unfortunate, the disappointed, here find 
equal relief; their tortured souls here find a balm for the 
deep and painful wounds they have received, and soon 
regain their pristine health and vigor. Sickness and 
affliction would fly with horror from retirement, if its 
friendly shades did not afford them tliat consolation which 
they are. unable to obtain in the resorts of feshion. The 
subtle vapor which sensuality and intoxication shed upon 
the objecto that surround a state of health and happiness, 
entirely disappear ; and all those charms which subsist 
rather in imagination than reality, lose their power. To 
the happy every object wears the delightful colors of the 
rose; but to the miserable all is blank and dreadful. 
The two conditions are equally in the extreme ; but they 
do not, in either case, discover the errors into which they 
are betrayed, until the curtain drops ; when the scene 
changes, and the illusion is dissipated. 

How unhappy should we bo, if Divine Providence 
were to grant us everything we desire ! Even under the 
Yery afflictions by which man believes his happiness to 
be destroyed, heaven may propose something extraordi- 
nary in his fevor. New circumstances excite new 
exertions. In solitude and tranquillity, if we earnestly 
endeavor to conquer misfortune, the activity of life, 
which, until the moment of adversity, had been perhaps 



OM THE AFPUCnOllS Or IIFX. 



297 



suspended, suddenly changes, and the mind regains its 
eneigy and vigor, eren while it laments the state of 
inaction, to whidi it conceives itself to be i r retri e vably 
reduced. 

If sorrow force us into retirement, patience and per- 
severance soon restore the soul to its natural tranquillity 
and happiness. We ought never to inspect the volume 
of futurity ; its pages will only deceive us ; on the con* 
trary, we ought forever to repeat this experimental truth, 
this consoUtory maxim,— that the objects which men 
behold at a distance with fear and trembling, lose, on a 
nearer approach, not only their disagreeable and menac- 
ing aspect, but firequently in the event, produce the most 
agreeable and unexpected pleasure. He who tries every 
expedient, who boldly opposes himsdf to every difficulty, 
who stands steady and inflexible to eveiy obstacle, who 
neglects no exertion within his power, and relies with 
confidence upon divine aid, extracts from afiliction both 
its poison and its sting, and deprives misfortune of its 
victory. 

The opportunity which a valetudinarian enjoys of 
employing his faculties with -fecility and success, in a 
manner conformable to the extent of his designs, is un- 
doubtedly short, and passes rapidly away. Such happi- 
ness is the lot only of those who enjoy robust health ; 
they alone can exclaim, ** Time is my own.*' But he 
who labors under continual sickness uid sufiering, and 
whose avocation depends on the public necessity or 
caprice, can never say that he has one moment to him- 
self. He must watch the fleeting hours as they pass, 
and seise an interval of leisure when and where he can. 
Necessity, as well as reason, convinces him, that he must, 
in spite of his daily sufierings, his wearied body, or his 
harassed mind, firmly resist his accumulating troubles^

the sonl from brooding over its torments and its sorrows, 
by inspiring the mind with ideas of yirtaoos sentiments, 
noble actions, and generous inclinations. For this reason 
it is necessary to cnltirate in our minds the lore of action, 
and after a dutiful and entire submission to the dispensa- 
tions of heaven, force ourselves into employment, untfl, 
from the warmth of our exertions, we acquire an habitual 
alertness. I consider a disposition to be active, amid 
that disgust and apathy which dry up the fountains ok 
lifo, as the most sure and efficacious antidote against the 
poison of a dejected spirit, a soured temper, or a melan- 
choly mind. 

The influence of the mind upon tlie body is one of the 
most consolatory truths, to those who are the subject of 
habitual suiferinQs. Supported by this idea, they never 
permit tlieir reason to be entirely overcome; religion, 
under this idea, never loses its powerful empire in tlie 
breast ; and they learn from experience, that-even in the 
extremity of distress, eveiy object which diverts the 
attention, softens the evils we endure, and frequently 
drives them unperceived away. 

Many celebrated philosophers have by this means at 
lengtli been able, not only to preserve a tranquil mind in 
the midst of the ihost poignant suiTorings, but have even 
increased tho strength of their intellectual faculties, in 
spite of tlicir misfortunes. Rous seau composed the greater 
part of h» immortal works under the continual pressure 
of sickness and of giiof. OeHort, who, by his mild, 
agrceablo, and instructive writings, became the preceptor 
of Germany, certainly found in this interesting occupa- 
tion, the surest remedy against melancholy. At an age 
already for advanced in lifo, Mendelsohm, who, though 
not by nature subject to dejection, was for a long time 
opprMsed by an almost inconceivable derangement of the

Tuns, the great limner of nature, who tints the hills 
and plains with rerdnre, and garnishes the rocks with 
Tariogated leafage, enhances all the charms of old familiar 
places. Scones tliat in the early period of youth were 
bleak and cheerless, seom, when timo has given them a 
place among our distant recollections, clothed with a 
lavish beauty ; filled with the light of brighter skies, and 
Tocal with sounds that are sweeter than music The 
human soul, capable of a variety of affliction and of 
solace, finds in each something to alleviate its burden. 
In the bosom of these scenes, we are transported in imagi- 
nation back to tills romantic stage in the journey of life ; 
the streams renew their ancient babbling, and return 
sweet responses to , our silent invocations ; and in the 
gleaming objects of the landscape, we behold the haxy 
pictures of memory resolved into bright realities. Every 
once fiimiliar object opens a separate page in our history ; 
and when we are absent from them, the book is closed, 
and all our retrospect is dark and moumfui. 

Man alone is sensible to those enjoyments tliat spring 
firom contemplating the scenes of past sorrows. Nature 
has given us this sentiment, which is allied to that of 
divinity, to raise us above our more physical being to the 
contemplation of suUimer themes. Hence there is always 
some religious fiiith — tliough it may bo unattended with 
rational assurance — accompanying the sentiment of 
pleasure tlutt springs from the remembrance of affliction. 
The benevolence of the Deity has converted all our 
sorrows into so many sources of happiness, causing them, 
after their poignancy is gone, to reflect back upon our

because it hiu no viroal attraction^ to divert ns from ihe 
pleasing suggestions of tlio imagination. It is for this 
reason that so many plain houses, plain tombs, and rude 
landscapes, have a charm in our sight which we cannot 
behold in otliers more tasteful and adorned. And 
hence almost all the hallowed* spots in our remembrance 
are simple and unartistic. It is on the rude rock that 
overlooks an unadorned prospect, in the old road that 
leads through a mass of tangled shrubbery, in the moss- 
grown cottage, and Uie rustic hamlet, where memory 
delights to dwell, and vrcave for us tlie weft of her inspi- 
ration, r V A 
The house in which I was bom, and where I lived 
during the period of childhood, has lost its original and 
endearing simplicity by a few alterations. These are just 
sufficient to deprive tlie place of a great part of the 
gacredness, with which I have always regarded it, as the 
scene of my eariicst recollections. The hollow, situated 
a few rods behind it, and which was then filled with a 
grove of locust trees, is no^rcut up into gardens ; and 
the gentle sloped so enchanting to the lovers of nature, 
has been terraced by the owners, for ornament and tlie 
convenience of tillage. The locust trees are all gone, and 
with them tlie beauty of tlie place has departed. I some- 
times look over the fence, and endeavor to bring back 
to my mind the whole scene, as it was when I first looked 
abroad upon the earth under these trees. Then I cannot 
avoid giving myself up to regrets, and lamenting the 
changes that are constantly depriving the scenes of our 
early life of their sacredness and their identity. 

Other objects about this place are not greatly altered. 
The next house, with iu old-fashioned garden, still re- 
mains in Its primitive condition. It has not yet been 
by improvements. When a child, I used to listen

to the cawing of crows, as I walked along the footpaUi on 
tlie Sidewalk that was bounded by this garden fence. I 
look forward, witli a gloomy anticipation, to the time 
when this place also will be modemiiwl, and the foot- 
path, that leads through tlie grassy sidewalk, will be 
covered with a neat spread of gravel, for tlie convenience 
of an increasing population. At present, Uiis whole side- 
walk is to me a consecrated spot ; and as I sometimes 
stroll along its patli, I listen for the cawing of crows that, 
strangely enough, are the only sounds which are vivid in 
my memor}' in connection with the scene. By a revival 
of tlieso memories, one is inspired witli a sense of that 
freshness of existence, which gave every object in life a 
brightness and beauty, that fade and become tarnished 
at a later period of our years. 

In vain do we endeavor to fix our aflections upon any 
new places as we are enamored with those of. other days. 
There cheering voices come sounding up from eveiyonce 
familiar nook and turn ; and the enlivening echo of re- 
membered joys falls like music on the ear. Lights tliat 
have a quaint, endearing lustre, gleam fondly from the 
cottage windows ; and in the still moonlight, die shadows 
present their wcll-remembcred forms with a startling 
fidelity, --la if time here had stayed his progress, and 
kindly waited to satisfy our lingering affections. As one 
grows older, these scenes bccomeso many remembrancers, 
reviving not only the recollections, but tlie very feelings 
and hopes that, early in life, relieved eveiy pUce of its 
insipidity: 

I am guilty of no egotism when I recount such i«col- 
lections : for these experiences of my life are those of 
every person of feeling. There is no one who does not 
cherish some spot in his native village and among the 
scenes of youthful freqnentation, as holy ground. Hen

same road — a distance of eighteen mfles — for the pleas- 
ure of reviewing the fields, the hoosos, the lakes, and the 
woods, which are now a beautiful chart of the scenes of 
this period of simple adventure. I am not a gloomy or 
an unhappy man ; but my happiness depends greatly on 
these things. My spirits are nurtured by a review of old 
accustomed places ; and my hopes are still bright as in 
youth, when my mind is lighted by the sunshine of the 
same fields in which it received its first lessons of nature 
and humanity. 

In every part of tliis old road, I meet tlie unages of my 
companions with whom I was associated in these pedes- 
trian journeys. How beautiful is every clump of vineiy 
that fiings its umbrage over fence and paUiway, and 
every green thicket that is mirrored upon the glassy pool 
beneath I And how like sacrilege seems the labor that 
has removed any one of them, for convenience or im- 
provement I I am greeted here by thoughts that never 
oome to me in any other place ; forms and visions of 
friends and of friendship which no other scene or pros^iect 
can awaken* Here they are enshrined by memory, mado 
visible among the shadows and the sunshine, and appear- 
ing in joyous wakefulness among the trees and flowers— 
fond messengers of past happiness whk^h tlie genius of tlio 
place alone can revoke. 

Memory is not wholly the result of a voluntary effort. 
The power of recollection depends greatly on suggestions 
from outward objects ; and if we would recall the events 
and feelings, the ideas and sensations of youth, we must 
visit the places where they first impressed our minds. 
Many a delightful fancy tlien rises to cheer the soul with 
the fVeshness of the morning of life, and many a hope wo 
thought was lost, comes to us, like an old firiend, with 
glad assurances of the future.

There u a. certain kind of melancholy wHtcli, ntiaUemd- 
ed with deepondcney, becomei a ftource of tlio purest

leutiment that is awakened by the sight of those object*

»icred lineaments. The world is entirely unchcerfuli 
when I dwell beyond access to these hnl lowed grounds ;

tentment nnd new inspimtionf to prescn'e the light of my 
loul until another review, It may be the inclosures of a 
dwelling-house ; a narrow Inne timt passes through a 
wood ; a hillside that overlooks the sea or the adjacent 
villages ; it may bo only a footjinth by the tide of a 
brook : — but it is something through which I must pass, 
to arrive at mj own pf^rodise. 

A perpetual fountain of delight wells up fi*om these 
Ifcnej of memory ; and when I see them changed by 
improvements, I mourn as over the grave of a fnend. 
For these are the jviges whereon is written the history 
ofourlifo; and with ever)' {Tld tree that is cut down, 
evciy umbrageous thicket tlmt is removed, and every 
ancient house that is modernized by the hand of taste, 
some bright page is torn from thi$ book of life. We hear 
mesAages of early friendnhip that come only fi-om the 
voices of these streams; music that accords but witli these 
rustling bouglts and foliage ; we behold beauty that re- 
vives only with these flowers ; Jove that wakes and weeps 
forgotten tears, never save among these dripping foun^ 
tattti and these echoing hills. Hence the wretchedness 
of a man of feeling who is an exile from his native land^ 
ind from the scenes of his early years. He sees many 
beautiful tnd pleasant places ; but no familiar deity ro- 
iidet in them i no memory haunts them* They ore mere 
bl&nks: like strangers^ they do not smile upon him, and 
be cannot lore their features.

MOVNT AUBUBN. 



So all things, as thej rise, and blush and bloom, 
Are seared, at last, and scattered for the tomb. 

Kew happy hosts, nnceasing, pass away ; 

For time, their pilot, suffers no delay. 

In toil and tamult, full of hope and trust, 

They rise and revel and return to dust ; 

Some dropping by the wayside in their prime ; - 

Some lingering, till forgotten in their time. 

While Providence stUl hides our journey's end — . 

Thus dreaming, hoping, joying, we descend. 

Like insects in our path tJiat creep or fly. 

Are bom and flutter, and grow old and die. 

But love and mate, and chaunt their life-long tune, 

In but three revolutions of the moon. 

We chase our object, leave it, and pursue 

A brighter vision opening on our view. 

Still other phantoms guide us and allure ; 

For tliese we hope, and baClle and endure. 

Our preparations are our daily feast ; 

The joys of our fruition are Uio least. 

The sounds of heaven, the bird*s, the insect's lay. 

The gems, tlie fruits, tlie flowers that gird our way ; - 

By these spell-bound, entranced, in sun and shower, 

We laugh and linger, till our last brief hour. 

Life, luring, glittering, still is but a chase : 

We drc^ our prixes, to pursue the race. 

Then comes the final act : our course is run ; 

The pageant disappears, and death is won. 

But let us not Ument this sad decree— 
This fate that stamped us with mortality. 
For there are mystic lighU in heaven that show 
Man's being ends not here in death and woe.

ahated ; and where its true aims and issnes are revealed, 
— it is t]ie place of graves. Here — how ohvious and

who hut as yesterday, were as active, as earnest, as en- 
grossed in ** tilings seen and temporal,'* as we are now.

pursuits of this present state, are with them all over. 
** The lust of the flesh, the lust of tlie eyes, and the pride 
of life,*' are all passed away. 






** Shall wo build to ambition ? — Ah no I 
Affrighted, ho shrinketh away ; 
For see, tliey would pin him below 
In a small, narrow cave, and begirt with cold clay, 
To the meanest of reptiles a peer and a proy." 



Envy has now censed to carp at the desert it could not 
rival; anger no longer bums; hatred forgets to plot; 
the '* itching palm of avarice is cold ; simulation is weary 
of feigning ; dissimulation is tired of concealing ; preten- 
sion has strutted its little hour ; vanity has achieved all 
Its petty triumphs ; and the busy tongue of calumny is 
still — lit last." And docs the night of death, we invol- 
untarily ask, so soon settle down upon the short day of 
human life ? And shall we plan long designs for a space 
so brief? Does not ratlier the simple tliought of the 
transientness of mere eartlily cares and emotions, which 
is thus pressed, as a cold weight upon the heart, at every 
step we take among the graves of the departed, serve to 
qualify and chasten betimes an overmastering interest 
in any present object ?

distance has separated, no absence chilled, no vicissitude 
shaken, no adversitj withered, no sickness weakened, and 
no decay impaired, should be cut off and destroyed for- 
ever ; and just at that moment too, when it had acquired 
its highest strength, and had gained its closest hold upon 
the heart. Thus wo mourn the departed as ** not lost, 
but gone before,** and linger over the memorials that 
aflRsction rears, as tokens of the living, rather than of the 
dead, as types and symbok of an imperishable love. 

Such are some of the high uses that burial-places, 
properiy arranged, are fitted to subserve. Wo believe 
that they are real and important It is their natural 
effect to call forth and perpetuate feelings and sentiments 
which refine and elevate Uie mind, and purify while they 
soften the heart ; to remove from spots, in themselves sad 
and painful, all unnecessary gloom ; and to gather around 
them those associations, which at the same time, serve to 
solemnise the soul, and render it tranquil, serene and 
hopeful. We do not believe these influences are wholly 
lost on any. We do not believe that even those, whose 
muids are the most pre-occupied, the inveterate world- 
ling, for example, or the slave of ambition, could visit a 
well-ordered and beautiful cemetery, like our own Mount 
Auburn, under circumstances propitious to the appropri- 
ate influences of tlie place, and not come away, for the 
time at least, sadder it may be, but wiser and better 
beings than they entered tliere. 

We only add, on this part of the subject, tliat the 
views of the burial of the dead« which we have thus been 
led to take, are illustrated by the attention which has 
always been given to this subject, among the people of 
all cUmes. From the earliest historical records we learn 
that this subject vras recognised as one of primary con-

Hms would I wiah to ilccp. — Tlii» n the spot 
Which I have long marked out to Iny my bones In | 
Tired out and wearied with the riotous worid, 
Beneath this yew I would bo sepulchred. 
It is a lovely spot I The sultry sun. 
From his meridian height, endeavors vainly 
To pierce the shadowy foliage, while the lophyr 
Comes wafting gently o'er tlie rippling Trent, 
And phiys about my cheek. It is a nook 
Most pleasant. Such a one, perchance, did Gray 
Frequent, as with a vagrant muse he wantoned. 
Come, I will sit me down and meditate. 
For I am wearied with my summer's walk ; 
And hero I may repose in silent case ; 
And thus, perchance, when life's sad journey's o'er, 
My harassed soul in this same spot, may find 
The haven of its rest — beneath this sod, 
• Pcnshftnce may slumber sweetly, sound as death. 

I would not have my corpse cemented down 
With brick and stone, defrauding the poor worm 
Of its predestined dues ; no, I would lie 
Beneath a little hillock, grass-o'ergrown. 
Swathed down with osiers, just as sleep the cotters. 
Yet may not undistittguUked be my grave; 
But there, at eve, may some congenial soul 
Duly resort, and shed a pious tear, 
Tlie good man's benison — no more I ask. 
And Oh I (if heavenly beings may look down 



UKB8. 

From Whore, with cherubim, inspired they sit. 
Upon this little dim-discovered spot 
The earth,) then will I cast a glance below 
On liim who thus my ashes shall embalm ; 
And I will weep too, and will bless the wandorar, 
Wishing ho may not long bo doomed to pino 
In Uiu low-thoughted world of darkling woe,— 
But that, ere long, he reach his kindred skies. 

Yet 'twas a silly thought, as if tho body. 
Mouldering beneath the surface of the eartli, 
Could taste tho sweets of summer scenery, 
And feel tlie freshness of tlie balmy breeas I 
Yet Nature speaks within the human bosom, 
And, spite of reason, bids it look beyond 
His narrow verge of being, and provide 
A decent residence for ite clayey shell. 
Endeared to it by time. And who would lay 
His body in the^jty burial placC] 
To bo thrown up again by some rude sexton. 
And yield its narrow house another tenant, 
Exposed to insult lewd, and wantonness ? 
No I I will lay me in tho village ground ; 
Tlicre are the dead respected. The poor hind, 
Unlettered as ho is, would scorn to invade 
The silent resting-place of death. I've seen 
The laborer returning from his toil. 
Here stay his steps, and call his children round, 
And slowly spell the rudely sculptured rhymes, 
And in his rustic manner moralize. 
I've marked with what a silent awe he spake, 
With head uncovered, his respectful manner, 
And all tlie honors which ho paid tlie grave, 
And thought on cities, where even cemeteries,

^ Christianity fully established, gave the final extinction 
to these sepulchral bonfires." The practice is supposed 
to havo had its origin in different causes. Some thought 
that the action of fire was necessary to purify the soul 
from its earthliness, so that it might return to its primal 
source. Otliers resorted to it, for the purpose of securing 
the remains of the dead from insult and outrage. *' To 
be gnawed out of our graves," says the autlior just 
quoted, ** to have our skulls made into drinking bowls, 
and our bones turned into pipes, to delight and sport our 
enemies, are magical abominations csaipcd in burning 
burials." Again, ** he that hath the aslies of a friend, 
hath an everlasting treasure ; when fire taketli leave, 
corruption slowly enters.'* There were some who were

of tlie dead. Among semi-barbarous people, they vary 
with almost every tribe ; while nations of a higher cul- 
ture have, almost without exception, confined themselves 
to Inhumt\tion, or Interment, in some of its various forms, 
or to Cremation and Urn-Burial. 

In connection with these modes of burial we refer, as 
briefly as possible, to some of the more remarkable rites 
and forms, in which these last offices have been performed. 
The earliest as well as fullest account, wo have of these, 
is that of Homer. But they did not differ materially 
from that ob^rvrd by the Romans, who, indeed, copied 
them from the former. We will omit them at present, 
to speak of the funeral rites of the Egyptians. These 
were vcr}' remarkable, and in some respects different 
from all others. Among them the following may be 
briefly referred to. When any one died, the females of 
the family, covering their heads and faces with mud, and 
leaving the body in . the house, ran throngh the streets, 
striking themselves and uttering loud lamentations. 
Hired mourners were employed to increase tlicso mani- 
festations of grief. Tlie body was then conveyed to tho 
erobalmors. The mourning family, during seventy-two 
days, continuod their lamentations at home, singing tlie

were laid upon r funeral pyre, or pile, which was simply

could not bo placed, according to a prohibition of tlio 
Twelve Tables, within sixty feet of any private dwelling ; 
and by a subsequent law, enacted in the time of Augus- 
tus, it was to bo removed, at least two miles from the city. 
On a pile like tliis, the dead body, togoUier witli the 
bier on which it had been carried— for it was customary

and not choice, imiiosed the task ; and the winds were

solemn mareh, tlirico rei»catod was, in some cas*^, niado

with tlio music of wmd instruments, in the case of illus- 
trious persons.

After tlie body had been sufficiently consumed, which 
was indicated by tlie gradual settling of the white ashes

was sprinkled on tlie embers. Next followed tlie collect-

alluded to by tlie festive poets of antiquity, intimating

burning bones nfter death, might bo more seasonably 
applied in moistening their living clay. This ofKce fell to 
the nearest friends; their hands were carefully purifiwl ;

fnend.

Cenotaphs, or empty monuments, as already intimated,

religiously adopted hy the Romans, that the ghosts of 
tlie departed would remain homeless and without a rest-

invoked, was huilt for them. The story of Palinurus, in 
the sixth hook of the JEneid, may he taken as an expo- 
nent of the common fiiith and feeling on this subject.
